Dense Ideals
Monroe Eskew, Yair Hayut

Abstract
In this paper, we obtain the consistency, relative to large cardinals, of the existence of dense ideals on every successor of a regular cardinal simultaneously. Using a consequent transfer principle, we show that in this model there is a σ \sigma -complete, ℵ 1 \aleph_{1} -dense ideal on ℵ n + 1 \aleph_{n+1} for every n < ω n<\omega , answering a question of Foreman. Using this construction we show the consistency of the existence of various irregular ultrafilters on ω n \omega_{n} , the consistency of the Foreman-Laver reflection property for the chromatic number of graphs for all possible pairs of cardinals below ℵ ω \aleph_{\omega} , and the simultaneous consistency of the partition hypotheses PH n ​ ( ω m ) \mathrm{PH}_{n}(\omega_{m}) for n < m n<m .



1
Introduction

A common philosophical stance in set theory is that a statement that follows from a large cardinal axiom is morally true, even if independent from the standard axioms of set theory,
\mathrm{ZFC}
. For example, the statement that there is no definable non-measurable subset of the reals (e.g. in the structure
\langle\mathbb{R};\mathbb{N},+,\cdot,exp\rangle
) is considered true even though it is false in
L
, as it follows from the existence of sufficiently many large cardinals.

Nevertheless, by the Levy-Solovay theorem, large cardinals are indifferent to small forcing such as the ones that can wiggle the value of the continuum, so large cardinals cannot decide whether the continuum hypothesis is true. In the paper
[
11
]
, Foreman suggests an alternative to the standard large cardinal hierarchy, the
generic
large cardinals. Those axioms are assertions about the existence of elementary embeddings in a generic extension of the universe. They are not linearly ordered as the standard large cardinal axioms are, but they can hold at small cardinals (such as
\aleph_{n}
), thus giving us information about the universe’s structure at those cardinals.
1
1

1

This paradigm has its limitations, see
[
8
]
.

The strength of an elementary embedding
j:V\to M
is measured by the similarity of the obtained model
M
to the universe
V
, relative to the critical point of
j
. For a generic elementary embedding, there is another important parameter: the forcing notion that introduces the elementary embedding. The more modest the forcing notion is, the stronger the generic large cardinal axiom becomes. To get an elementary embedding with a critical point
\mu^{+}
, where
\mu
is a cardinal,
\mu^{+}
must be collapsed. When
\mu
is regular, the Levy collapse
\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\mu^{+})
is the most natural candidate for a forcing notion that minimizes the modification of the universe in this case.

Similarly to the connection between
\sigma
-complete ultrafilters and definable elementary embeddings, there is a tight connection between ideals with various strong combinatorial properties and generic elementary embeddings. For an ideal
I
on a set
X
, the forcing notion
\mathcal{P}(X)/I
introduces a generic elementary embedding as an ultrapower using the generic filter for the forcing.

Unlike traditional large cardinal axioms, generic large cardinal axioms can be mutually inconsistent. Thus, one of the goals in the study of generic large cardinals and ideals with strong properties is to isolate the limitations for the co-existence of such ideals at many small regular cardinals.

The main theorem of this paper is Theorem
56
.

Theorem

(Main Theorem)
.

It is consistent, relative to a huge cardinal, that for every regular cardinal
\mu
, there is a normal ideal
I
on
\mu^{+}
such that

\mathcal{P}(\mu^{+})/I\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\mu^{+})).

By Proposition
57
, the existence of such ideals implies
\mathrm{GCH}
, which implies that the forcing
\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\mu^{+})
has cardinality
\mu^{+}
. We conclude that the ideal
I
on
\mu^{+}
is
\mu^{+}
-dense.

This result is optimal as it obtains the maximal class of successor cardinals which can carry a dense ideal. For a singular cardinal
\mu
, there can be no
\mu^{+}
-dense, normal ideal on
\mu^{+}
, by
[
8
, Lemma 2]
.

Following the terminology of
[
14
, Section 11.4]
, our main theorem shows that it is consistent for every successor of a regular cardinal to be
minimally generically almost-huge
.
2
2

2

This means that for
\kappa=\mu^{+}
, the forcing
\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\kappa)
introduces an elementary embedding
j\colon V\to M
, where
M
is a
{<}j(\kappa)
-closed transitive subclass of the generic extension
V[G]
.
Our result cannot be improved to get any one cardinal minimally generically huge, as minimal generic hugeness of
\kappa
implies that
\kappa^{+}
is not generically measurable via a
\kappa
-closed forcing, by
[
9
, Theorem 1]
.

Using the Boolean transfer principle (see Subsection
7.2
), we obtain Corollary
66
:

Corollary

(Main Corollary)
.

It is consistent, relative to a huge cardinal, that for every
n
, there is a uniform,
\sigma
-complete,
\omega_{1}
-dense ideal on
\omega_{n+1}
.

This corollary has many combinatorial consequences, and we devote Section
7
to stating some of them.

Our results answer the following open questions from the literature:

•

The Main Corollary answers
[
14
, Question 15]
.

•

Theorem
68
obtains all possible values, subject to the constraints of
\mathrm{GCH}
, for sizes of ultrapowers among the
\omega_{n}
’s, answering
[
14
, Question 16]
below
\aleph_{\omega}
.
3
3

3

Other parts of Question 16 were answered in the first author’s thesis.

•

The Main Theorem implies that it is consistent to have very strongly layered ideals on all
\omega_{n}
simultaneously, answering
[
14
, Question 17]
.

•

The Main Corollary also shows that Foreman’s computation of the forbidden segments is sharp in the lower part, as asked in
[
14
, Question 34]
.
4
4

4

In Subsection
7.4
, we improve the computation of the forbidden segments, pointing to a more accurate formulation of Question 34, which remains open.

•

In Subsection
7.5
, we derive as a corollary from the existence of uniform dense ideals the minimal possible chromatic number for the Erdős-Hajnal graphs, under
\mathrm{GCH}
, for
\omega_{n}
,
n<\omega
, addressing a problem of Todorćevič,
[
30
, Problem 8.17]
.

•

In Subsection
7.6
, we obtain an upper bound for the consistency strength of the partition hypotheses of
[
2
]
, addressing Question 9.5 of that work.

Let us describe the structure of the paper.
In Section
2
, we will cover the basic technical properties of forcings, projections, and embeddings needed for the definition of our main forcing notion.

In Section
3
, we introduce the main technical tool of this paper, which is the
Dual Shioya collapse
. This forcing notion is a minor variant of one developed by Shioya in
[
27
]
. It collapses the interval between a pair of uncountable regular cardinals
\mu
and
\kappa
in a relatively universal way. Intuitively, the forcing glues together generically arbitrary
\mu
-closed forcing notions of cardinality
<\kappa
.

In order to lift an elementary embedding after the Dual Shioya collapse, we need to make sure that many steps of the generic collapse behave as inverse limits, a property that generically fails quite often. In Section
4
, we introduce a forcing notion that will allow us to restore a large set on which inverse limits exist, which will be crucial in order to construct the required master conditions.

In Section
5
, we prove the consistency of the simultaneous existence of dense ideals at
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
relative to a suitable large cardinal hypothesis. This is an improvement on Foreman’s result
[
12
]
, where he obtained a dense ideal on
\omega_{1}
together with a very strongly layered ideal
\omega_{2}
, which he showed has many of the same combinatorial consequences at the level of
\omega_{2}
.
The construction of the ideal for
\omega_{1}
differs from the construction of a dense ideal on
\mu^{+}
for uncountable regular
\mu
. The reason for that is that the Dual Shioya collapse is not well-defined when the lower cardinal is
\omega
. Luckily, the problem of constructing dense ideals on
\omega_{1}
was already solved in several ways, some of which are compatible with the rest of the construction.

In Section
6
we prove our main consistency result by showing how to lift an almost-huge embedding after a two-step iteration of the forcing from Section
3
and Section
4
.

In Section
7
, we derive a couple of
\mathrm{ZFC}
-consequences from the existence of ideals as in the Main Theorem. This section
does not require any knowledge about the results of the other sections and can be read independently.
We start by obtaining some simple consequences of these ideals and then, in Subsection
7.2
we recall the principle of Boolean transfer that was developed by Foreman and Woodin and apply it in our case in order to get a variety of ideals of different density and completeness over
\omega_{n}
for natural
n
. In
[
12
]
, Foreman developed a strong and general version of this transfer principle which applies to many types of ideals. We present a simplified version of the proof which is suitable for our case. Using the Boolean transfer principle, we repeat Foreman’s argument for our ideals and obtain in Subsection
7.3
highly irregular ultrafilters. In Subsection
7.4
, we present an improved argument for the computation of a class of regular cardinals on which no
\sigma
-complete
\omega_{2}
-saturated ideal can exist.

In Subsection
7.5
, we use those irregular ultrafilters in order to compute the chromatic number of the Erdős-Hajnal graph for
\omega_{n}^{\omega_{m}}
and obtain the Foreman-Laver reflection principle for the chromatic number of graphs between all possible pairs of cardinals below
\aleph_{\omega}
. Finally, in Subsection
7.6
, we derive the consistency of the Partition Hypothesis of
[
2
]
and conclude (using an unpublished work,
[
3
]
) the consistency of the triviality of the cohomology group
\hat{\mathrm{H}}^{n}(\omega_{m},\mathcal{A})
for
n<m
.

In Section
8
, we list some open problems.

Throughout the paper we work in
\mathrm{ZFC}
and state explicitly any large cardinal hypothesis that we are using. We refer the reader to
[
18
]
for basic facts about forcing and to
[
19
]
for definitions and basic properties of large cardinals.

2
Preliminaries

Let us first recall a few basic forcing facts. A partially ordered set (poset)
\mathbb{P}
is called
separative
when for all
p,q\in\mathbb{P}
, if
p\nleq q
, then there is
r\leq p
such that
r\perp q
, i.e. there is no element below both
r
and
q
. The key property of separative posets is that
p\leq q
if and only if
p\Vdash q\in\dot{G}
, where
\dot{G}
is the canonical name for the generic filter. A non-separative poset may be made separative by forming the
seperative quotient
, where we put
p\preceq q
iff all
r\leq p
are compatible with
q
and defining the equivalence relation
p\sim q\leftrightarrow p\preceq q\preceq p
. A generic for a poset can be readily translated into one for its separative quotient and vice versa. Thus, we will implicitly identify a poset and its separative quotient, unless it can cause confusion.

If
\mathbb{P}
is a poset and
\dot{\mathbb{Q}}
is a
\mathbb{P}
-name for a poset, then the iteration
\mathbb{P}*\dot{\mathbb{Q}}
is taken to be the set of pairs
\langle p,\dot{q}\rangle\in H_{\theta}
, where
\theta
is the least regular cardinal such that
\mathbb{P},\dot{\mathbb{Q}}\in H_{\theta}
, such that
\mathbb{P}
forces
\dot{q}\in\dot{\mathbb{Q}}
, with the order defined as
\langle p_{1},\dot{q}_{1}\rangle\leq\langle p_{0},\dot{q}_{0}\rangle
iff
p_{1}\leq p_{0}
and
p_{1}\Vdash\dot{q}_{1}\leq\dot{q}_{0}
. This ordering is usually not antisymmetric, but it becomes a partial order when we mod out by the equivalence relation
\langle p_{1},\dot{q}_{1}\rangle\sim\langle p_{0},\dot{q}_{0}\rangle
iff
p_{0}=p_{1}
and
p_{0}\Vdash\dot{q}_{0}=\dot{q}_{1}
. We officially take this quotient as our definition of two-step iteration, but we will suppress mention of it for notational convenience. It is easy to see that this poset is separative if
\mathbb{P}
is separative and
\dot{\mathbb{Q}}
is forced to be separative.

There are two main ways that one forcing can be thought of as a subforcing of another: projections and complete embeddings.

Definition 1
.

For posets
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
, a map
\pi:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{P}
is a
projection
when it is order-preserving, its range is dense in
\mathbb{P}
, and it has the property that for all
q\in\mathbb{Q}
and all
p\leq\pi(q)
, there is
q^{\prime}\leq q
such that
\pi(q^{\prime})\leq p
.

The statement that
\ran\pi
is dense in
\mathbb{P}
is made superfluous by requiring that
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
have maximum elements
1_{\mathbb{P}}
and
1_{\mathbb{Q}}
respectively, and
\pi(1_{\mathbb{Q}})=1_{\mathbb{P}}
. If
\mathbb{P}
or
\mathbb{Q}
is missing a maximum element, then these can be artificially added and a given projection can be extended to map one to the other. We also note that if
\mathbb{P}
is separative,
\mathbb{Q}
has a maximum element
1_{\mathbb{Q}}
, and
\pi:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{P}
is a projection in the above sense, then
\pi(1_{\mathbb{Q}})
must be a maximum element of
\mathbb{P}
.

The above notion of projection is weaker than what is often used in the literature (for example in
[
1
]
), as we do not require that whenever
p\leq\pi(q)
, we can find
q^{\prime}\leq q
with
\pi(q^{\prime})=p
. A key advantage of using the weaker notion is that, if
D\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
is dense, then
\pi\restriction D
is also a projection from
D
to
\mathbb{P}
. And this weaker notion still does the usual jobs: If
\pi:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{P}
is a projection and
H\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
is generic, then
\pi[H]
generates a generic filter for
\mathbb{P}
, which we denote by
\pi(H)
. Forcing with
\mathbb{Q}
is equivalent to first taking a generic
G\subseteq\mathbb{P}
and then forcing with the quotient
\mathbb{Q}/G=\pi^{-1}[G].

A map
e:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{Q}
is a
complete embedding
when it preserves order and incompatibility and sends maximal antichains to maximal antichains. Such a map is called a
dense embedding
when its range is dense in
\mathbb{Q}
. If
e:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{Q}
is a complete embedding, then forcing with
\mathbb{Q}
is equivalent to first taking a generic
G\subseteq\mathbb{P}
and then forcing with the quotient
\mathbb{Q}/G=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}:q
is compatible with
e(p)
for all
p\in G\}
.
For any separative poset
\mathbb{P}
, we can form the Boolean completion
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{P})
, the complete Boolean algebra of regular open subsets of
\mathbb{P}
, in which
\mathbb{P}
appears as a dense set. For complete Boolean algebras
\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}
, the existence of a projection
\pi:\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{A}
is equivalent to the existence of a complete embedding
e:\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
.

For ordinals
\kappa
and
\lambda
,
\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\lambda)
is the collection of partial functions from
\kappa
to
\lambda
defined at
{<}\kappa
-many points, ordered by reverse inclusion.
\mathrm{Add}(\kappa)
is
\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,2)
.
\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)
is the collection of partial functions
p
from
\lambda\times\kappa
to
\lambda
defined at
{<}\kappa
-many points, such that for all
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\in\dom p
,
p(\alpha,\beta)<\alpha
. This is isomorphic to the
{<}\kappa
-support product of
\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\alpha)
for
\alpha<\lambda
. For
\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}
, there is a natural projection from
\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda^{\prime})
to
\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)
given by
p\mapsto p\restriction(\lambda\times\kappa)
. For notational simplicity, we will usually write
p\restriction\lambda
for this operation.

Let us recall the notions of strategic closure.
For a poset
\mathbb{P}
and an ordinal
\delta
, we define two games
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{I}}_{\delta}(\mathbb{P})
and
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{II}}_{\delta}(\mathbb{P})
. Two players alternate playing elements of
\mathbb{P}
in a descending sequence, with Player I making the first move. At limit stages, Player I plays first in
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{I}}_{\delta}(\mathbb{P})
, and Player II plays first in
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{II}}_{\delta}(\mathbb{P})
. Player II wins if the game lasts for
\delta
-many rounds; otherwise, Player I wins. For a cardinal
\kappa
, we say that
\mathbb{P}
is
\kappa
-strategically closed
if Player II has a winning strategy for
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{II}}_{\kappa}(\mathbb{P})
, and
strongly
\kappa
-strategically closed
if Player II has a winning strategy for
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{I}}_{\kappa}(\mathbb{P})
.

We will use the following variation on Easton’s Lemma:

Lemma 2
.

Suppose
\kappa
is a regular cardinal and
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}
are posets such that
|\mathbb{P}|<\kappa
and
\mathbb{Q}
is
\kappa
-distributive. Then
\mathbb{P}
preserves that
\mathbb{Q}
is
\kappa
-distributive.

Proof.

Suppose
G\times H\subseteq\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{Q}
is generic. Let
f\in V[G][H]
be a function from some
\alpha<\kappa
to the ordinals. Let
\tau\in V[H]
be a
\mathbb{P}
-name such that
\tau^{G}=f
. Since
|\mathbb{P}|<\kappa
, we may assume
\tau\in V
. Thus
\tau^{G}\in V[G]
.
∎

2.1
Complete
\kappa
-closure

Throughout this subsection,
\kappa
is a regular uncountable cardinal.

Definition 3
.

A poset is called
completely
\kappa
-closed
when all descending sequences of length
<\kappa
have a greatest lower bound or infimum (glb or inf).

Definition 4
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P}
is a completely
\kappa
-closed poset.
A set
X\subseteq\mathbb{P}
is called
\kappa
-closed
when for all
\delta<\kappa
and all descending sequences
\langle p_{i}:i<\delta\rangle\subseteq X
,
\inf_{i}p_{i}\in X
.

Definition 5
.

If
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}
are completely
\kappa
-closed posets, then we say an order-preserving map
\pi:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{Q}
is
\kappa
-continuous
when for all
\delta<\kappa
and all descending sequences
\langle p_{i}:i<\delta\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{P}
,
\pi(\inf_{i}p_{i})=\inf_{i}\pi(p_{i})
.

Lemma 6

(Folklore)
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P}
is a completely
\kappa
-closed poset. Then every directed
D\in[\mathbb{P}]^{<\kappa}
has a greatest lower bound.

Proof.

We argue by induction on the cardinality
\delta
of directed subsets of
\mathbb{P}
. For
\delta=\omega
, given a directed
D\subseteq\mathbb{P}
, we select a descending sequence
\langle p_{i}:i<\omega\rangle\subseteq D
such that for every
d\in D
, there is
i<\omega
such that
p_{i}\leq d
. Then
\inf_{i}p_{i}=\inf(D)
. If the hypothesis holds for all
\gamma<\delta
, let
D
be a directed set of size
\delta
, and let
\langle D_{\alpha}:\alpha<\delta\rangle
be a
\subseteq
-increasing continuous sequence of subsets of
D
such that each
D_{\alpha}
is directed and of size
<\delta
. By induction, each
D_{\alpha}
has a glb,
p_{\alpha}
. The descending sequence
\langle p_{\alpha}:\alpha<\delta\rangle
has a glb,
p_{\delta}
, which is a lower bound to
D
. Any other lower bound of
D
must be a lower bound of each
D_{\alpha}
and thus below each
p_{\alpha}
, so
p_{\delta}=\inf(D)
.
∎

Lemma 7
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}
are completely
\kappa
-closed posets and
\pi:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{Q}
is a
\kappa
-continuous order-preserving map. Then for all directed
D\in[\mathbb{P}]^{<\kappa}
,
\pi(\inf D)=\inf\pi[D]
.

Proof.

If
D
is countable, then there is a chain
C\subseteq D
such that for all
d\in D
, there is
c\in C
with
c\leq d
, so
\inf C=\inf D
and
\inf\pi[C]=\inf\pi[D]
. By assumption,
\pi(\inf C)=\inf\pi[C]
.

Suppose
\delta<\kappa
and the claim holds for all directed
D
of cardinality
<\delta
. Let
D
be hypothesized, and let
\langle D_{\alpha}:\alpha<\delta\rangle
be a continuous increasing sequence of sets such that each
D_{\alpha}
is a directed subset of
D
of size
<\delta
. Let
d_{\alpha}=\inf D_{\alpha}
. By induction,
\pi(d_{\alpha})=\inf\pi[D_{\alpha}]
. We have that
\inf D=\inf_{\alpha<\delta}d_{\alpha}
, and by
\kappa
-continuity for chains,
\pi(\inf D)=\inf_{\alpha<\delta}\pi(d_{\alpha})=\inf_{\alpha<\delta}(\inf\pi[D_{\alpha}])=\inf\pi[D]
.
∎

Lemma 8
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P}
is a completely
\kappa
-closed poset. If
\delta<\kappa
is a cardinal and
\{D_{\alpha}:\alpha<\delta\}
is a collection of
\kappa
-closed dense subsets of
\mathbb{P}
, then
\bigcap_{\alpha<\delta}D_{\alpha}
is
\kappa
-closed and dense.

Proof.

The intersection is easily seen to be
\kappa
-closed, by the
\kappa
-closure of each
D_{\alpha}
. To show density, first, let us argue for the case
\delta=2
. Let
p\in\mathbb{P}
be arbitrary. We choose a descending sequence
p_{0}\geq p_{1}\geq p_{2}\geq\dots
below
p
such that
p_{i}\in D_{0}
for even
i
and
p_{i}\in D_{1}
for odd
i
. By the closure of
D_{0}
and
D_{1}
,
p_{\omega}=\inf_{i}p_{i}\in D_{0}\cap D_{1}
.
It follows that the intersection of finitely many
\kappa
-closed dense sets is
\kappa
-closed and dense.

For infinite
\delta
, we argue by induction. Suppose the claim holds for all
\gamma<\delta
. Given
p_{0}\in\mathbb{P}
, we construct a descending sequence
\langle p_{\alpha}:\alpha\leq\delta\rangle
such that
p_{\alpha+1}\in\bigcap_{i\leq\alpha}D_{i}
, and for limit
\alpha
,
p_{\alpha}=\inf_{i<\alpha}p_{i}
. The successor step is done using the induction hypothesis. If
\alpha
is a limit, then for each
\beta<\alpha
,
\{p_{i}:\beta<i<\alpha\}\subseteq D_{\beta}
, and so
p_{\alpha}\in D_{\beta}
by
\kappa
-closure. It follows that
p_{\delta}\in\bigcap_{\alpha<\delta}D_{\alpha}
.
∎

Lemma 9
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}
are completely
\kappa
-closed posets,
D
is a
\kappa
-closed dense subset of
\mathbb{Q}
, and
\pi:D\to\mathbb{P}
is a
\kappa
-continuous projection. Then for each
\kappa
-closed dense
E\subseteq\mathbb{P}
,
\pi^{-1}[E]
is a
\kappa
-closed dense subset of
\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

To show closure, suppose
\delta<\kappa
and
\langle p_{\alpha}:\alpha<\delta\rangle
is a descending sequence contained in
D
such that
\pi(p_{\alpha})\in E
for all
\alpha<\delta
. By the closure of
D
,
p_{\delta}=\inf_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}\in D
, and by the continuity and order-preservation of
\pi
and the closure of
E
,
\pi(p_{\delta})=\inf_{\alpha}\pi(p_{\alpha})\in E
.

To show density, let
q_{0}\in D
. Let
p_{0}\leq\pi(q_{0})
be in
E
, and let
q_{1}\leq q_{0}
be in
D
and such that
\pi(q_{1})\leq p_{0}
. Continue in this manner, building descending sequences
p_{0}\geq p_{1}\geq p_{2}\geq\dots
and
q_{0}\geq q_{1}\geq q_{2}\geq\dots
such that
p_{i}\in E
,
q_{i}\in D
, and
\pi(q_{i})\geq p_{i}\geq\pi(q_{i+1})
. Then
\pi(\inf_{i}q_{i})=\inf_{i}\pi(q_{i})=\inf_{i}p_{i}\in E
.
∎

Lemma 10
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P}_{0},\mathbb{P}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{2}
are completely
\kappa
-closed posets, and for
i<2
,
D_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{i}
is a
\kappa
-closed dense subset, and
\pi_{i}:D_{i}\to\mathbb{P}_{i+1}
is a
\kappa
-continuous projection. Then there is a
\kappa
-closed dense
E\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{0}
on which
\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{0}
is defined and is a
\kappa
-continuous projection.

Proof.

Let
E=\pi_{0}^{-1}[D_{1}]
. Then
E
is dense and
\kappa
-closed by Lemma
9
, and
\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{0}
is defined on
E
. It is clear that
\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{0}
is order-preserving and
\kappa
-continuous. To show that it is a projection, let
p_{0}\in E
and let
p_{2}\leq\pi_{1}(\pi_{0}(p_{0}))
. Let
p_{1}\leq\pi_{0}(p_{0})
be such that
\pi_{1}(p_{1})\leq p_{2}
. Let
p_{0}^{\prime}\leq p_{0}
be such that
\pi_{0}(p_{0}^{\prime})\leq p_{1}
. Then
\pi_{1}(\pi_{0}(p_{0}^{\prime}))\leq\pi_{1}(p_{1})\leq p_{2}
.
∎

The following theorem is true for every regular cardinal
\kappa
. It is attributed to McAloon in
[
17
, Section 4, Theorem 1]
for the case
\kappa=\omega
. The uncountable case can be proved by combining McAloon’s proof and the ideas in the proof of
[
24
, Lemma 3]
.

A proof, under an additional separativity assumption on
\mathbb{P}
, also appears in
[
4
]
. We give the proof, adjusted to the non-separative case, for completeness. A similar argument is used in the proof of Lemma
43
.

Theorem 11
.

If
\mathbb{P}
is a
\kappa
-closed poset of size
\lambda>\kappa
that collapses
\lambda
to
\kappa
, then there is a dense embedding from
\{q\in\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\lambda):\dom q
is a successor ordinal
\}
to
\mathbb{P}
. If
\mathbb{P}
is completely
\kappa
-closed, then there is a
\kappa
-continuous dense embedding from
\{q\in\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\lambda):\dom q
is an ordinal
\}
to
\mathbb{P}
.

Proof.

Let
\dot{f}
be a
\mathbb{P}
-name for a surjection from
\kappa
to the generic filter
\dot{G}
. We build a tree
T\subseteq\mathbb{P}
of height
\kappa
with the following properties:

1.

1_{\mathbb{P}}
is the root of
T
.

2.

For each
t\in T
, there is a set
S(t)\subseteq T
of size
\lambda
such that for each
s\in S(t)

s<t
, and there is no
x\in T
such that
s<x<t
.

3.

For each descending chain
\langle t_{i}:i<\delta\rangle
in
T
, where
\delta<\kappa
, if
\inf_{i}t_{i}
exists in
\mathbb{P}
, then
\inf_{i}t_{i}\in T
. Otherwise, there is a set of
\lambda
-many pairwise incompatible lower bounds in
T
to
\langle t_{i}:i<\delta\rangle
, all of which are maximal in
T
among such lower bounds.

4.

T
is dense in
\mathbb{P}
.

This suffices to build the desired dense embedding. We build
T
inductively by levels,
\langle T_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
, where
T_{\alpha}
is the set of all
t\in T
such that the chain in
T
above
t
has length
\alpha
.

Suppose we have
\langle T_{i}:i<\alpha\rangle
, and inductively assume each
T_{i}
is a maximal antichain in
\mathbb{P}
. If
\alpha=\beta+1
, then for each
t\in T_{\beta}
, there are densely many
p\leq t
such that for some
q\in\mathbb{P}
,
p\Vdash\dot{f}(\check{\beta})=\check{q}
, and
p\leq q
. This is because, for any
p\leq t
, there is some
p^{\prime}\leq p
and some
q
such that
p^{\prime}\Vdash\dot{f}(\check{\beta})=\check{q}
, and since
p^{\prime}
forces that both itself and
q
are in the generic filter, we can take
p^{\prime\prime}\leq p^{\prime},q
. Pick a maximal antichain
A_{t}
of such
p
of size
\lambda
, which is possible since
\mathbb{P}
is nowhere
\lambda
-c.c. Let
T_{\alpha}=\bigcup\{A_{t}:t\in T_{\beta}\}
.

If
\alpha
is a limit, then for all chains
\vec{c}=\langle t_{i}:i<\alpha\rangle
where
t_{i}\in T_{i}
, if
\inf_{i}t_{i}
exists, let
A_{\vec{c}}=\{\inf_{i}t_{i}\}
, and otherwise choose a maximal antichain
A_{\vec{c}}
of size
\lambda
of lower bounds to
\langle t_{i}:i<\alpha\rangle
. Let
T_{\alpha}=\bigcup\{A_{\vec{c}}:\vec{c}
is a chain through
\langle T_{i}:i<\alpha\rangle\}
. To verify that
T_{\alpha}
is a maximal antichain, let
p\in\mathbb{P}
. Recursively choose descending sequences
\langle p_{i}:i<\alpha\rangle
and
\langle t_{i}:i<\alpha\rangle
such that
p_{0}=p
, and for all
i<\alpha
,
t_{i}\in T_{i}
and
p_{i+1}\leq p_{i},t_{i}
, using the assumption that each
T_{i}
is a maximal antichain. Then there is a lower bound
p_{\alpha}
to the sequences
\langle p_{i}:i<\alpha\rangle
and
\langle t_{i}:i<\alpha\rangle
, and
p_{\alpha}
is compatible with some
t\in T_{\alpha}
.

To show that
T
is dense in
\mathbb{P}
, let
p\in\mathbb{P}
. Let
q\leq p
be such that for some
\alpha<\kappa
,
q\Vdash\dot{f}(\check{\alpha})=\check{p}
. Let
t\in T_{\alpha+1}
be such that
q
is compatible with
t
. Then for some
r\in\mathbb{P}
,
t\Vdash\dot{f}(\check{\alpha})=\check{r}
and
t\leq r
. Thus some condition forces
\dot{f}(\check{\alpha})=\check{p}=\check{r}
, and therefore
p=r\geq t\in T
.
∎

Lemma 12
.

Suppose
\kappa
is a regular uncountable cardinal,

\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are completely
\kappa
-closed posets,
|\mathbb{Q}|=\lambda>|\mathbb{P}|
,
\mathbb{Q}
collapses
\lambda
to
\kappa
, and
\pi:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{P}
is a
\kappa
-continuous projection.

Then, there is a
\kappa
-closed dense set
D\subseteq\mathbb{P}\times\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\lambda)
and a
\kappa
-continous dense embedding
e:D\to\mathbb{Q}
with the property that
\pi(e(p,c))=p
for all
\langle p,c\rangle\in D
.

Proof.

Whenever
G\subseteq\mathbb{P}
is generic,
\pi^{-1}[G]
has a
\kappa
-closed dense subset isomorphic to the tree of conditions in
\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\lambda)
whose domain is an ordinal. This is because
\pi
is
\kappa
-continuous, so if
\delta<\kappa
and
\langle q_{i}:i<\delta\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
is a descending sequence such that
\pi(q_{i})\in G
, then
\pi(\inf_{i<\delta}q_{i})\in G
as well, implying that
\mathbb{Q}_{n}/G
is completely
\kappa
-closed. By Theorem
11
, in
V[G]
there is a
\kappa
-continuous dense embedding
\varphi:\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\lambda)\to\mathbb{Q}/G
. Let
\dot{\varphi}
be a name for such an embedding.

For
\langle p,c\rangle\in\mathbb{P}\times\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\lambda)
, if there is
q\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
p=\pi(q)
and
p\Vdash\dot{\varphi}(\check{c})=\check{q}
, then let
e(p,c)=q
. Since
\dot{\varphi}
is forced to be a function, such a
q
is unique if it exists. Since
\dot{\varphi}
is forced to be order-preserving,
e
is also order-preserving. If
e(p_{1},c_{1})=q_{1}\leq e(p_{0},c_{0})=q_{0}
, then
\pi(q_{1})=p_{1}\leq\pi(q_{0})=p_{0}
, and since
p_{1}\Vdash\dot{\varphi}(\check{c}_{1})=\check{q}_{1}\leq\check{q}_{0}=\dot{\varphi}(\check{c}_{0})
, we must have
c_{1}\leq c_{0}
. Thus
e
is an order-isomorphism with its range. It is easy to see that the domain of definition of
e
is
\kappa
-closed, and
e
is
\kappa
-continuous.

Let us show that the domain of definition of
e
is dense. Let
\langle p_{0},c_{0}\rangle\in\mathbb{P}\times\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\lambda)
be arbitrary. There is some
p_{0}^{\prime}\leq p_{0}
and some
q_{0}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
p_{0}^{\prime}\Vdash\check{q}_{0}\in\check{\mathbb{Q}}/\dot{G}
and
\dot{\varphi}(\check{c}_{0})=\check{q}_{0}
.
Since
p_{0}^{\prime}
and
\pi(q_{0})
are compatible, there is
q_{0}^{\prime}\leq q_{0}
such that
\pi(q_{0}^{\prime})\leq p_{0}^{\prime}
.
Then we can find
p_{1}\leq p_{0}^{\prime}
,
c_{1}\leq c_{0}
, and
q_{1}\leq q_{0}^{\prime}
such that
p_{1}\Vdash\dot{\varphi}(\check{c}_{1})=\check{q}_{1}
, and
p_{1}\leq\pi(q_{1})
.
Then we can find
q_{1}^{\prime}\leq q_{1}
such that
\pi(q_{1}^{\prime})\leq p_{1}
, and subsequently find
p_{2}\leq\pi(q_{1}^{\prime})
,
c_{2}\leq c_{1}
, and
q_{2}\leq q_{1}^{\prime}
such that
p_{2}\Vdash\dot{\varphi}(\check{c}_{2})=\check{q}_{2}
, and
p_{2}\leq\pi(q_{2})
.
Continue in this way, constructing sequences
p_{0}\geq p_{1}\geq\dots
,
c_{0}\geq c_{1}\geq\dots
, and
q_{0}\geq q_{1}\geq\dots
, so that for
i\geq 1
,
p_{i}\Vdash\dot{\varphi}(\check{c}_{i})=\check{q}_{i}
and
p_{i+1}\leq\pi(q_{i+1})\leq p_{i}
. Define
p_{\omega}=\inf_{i}p_{i}
,
c_{\omega}=\inf_{i}c_{i}
, and
q_{\omega}=\inf_{i}q_{i}
. By the continuity of
\pi
and
\dot{\varphi}
,
p_{\omega}\Vdash\dot{\varphi}(\check{c}_{\omega})=\check{q}_{\omega}
, and
\pi(q_{\omega})=p_{\omega}
.

Finally, let us show that the range of
e
is dense. Let
q\in\mathbb{Q}
be arbitrary. There is
\langle p_{0},c_{0}\rangle\in\mathbb{P}\times\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\lambda)
and
q_{0}\leq q
such that
p_{0}\Vdash\dot{\varphi}(\check{c}_{0})=\check{q}_{0}
.
Then find
q_{0}^{\prime}\leq q_{0}
such that
\pi(q_{0}^{\prime})\leq p_{0}
, and subsequently find
p_{1}\leq\pi(q_{0}^{\prime})
,
c_{1}\leq c_{0}
, and
q_{1}\leq q_{0}^{\prime}
such that
p_{1}\Vdash\dot{\varphi}(\check{c}_{1})=\check{q}_{1}
, and
p_{1}\leq\pi(q_{1})
.
Continue in this way, constructing sequences
p_{0}\geq p_{1}\geq\dots
,
c_{0}\geq c_{1}\geq\dots
, and
q_{0}\geq q_{1}\geq\dots
, such that
p_{i}\Vdash\dot{\varphi}_{n}(\check{c}_{i})=\check{q}_{i}
, and
p_{i+1}\leq\pi(q_{i+1})\leq p_{i}
. Define
p_{\omega}=\inf_{i}p_{i}
,
c_{\omega}=\inf_{i}c_{i}
, and
q_{\omega}=\inf_{i}q_{i}\leq q
. By the continuity of
\pi
and
\dot{\varphi}
,
p_{\omega}\Vdash\dot{\varphi}(\check{c}_{\omega})=\check{q}_{\omega}
, and
\pi(q_{\omega})=p_{\omega}
.
∎

2.2
Inverse limits

Suppose
\vec{\mathbb{P}}=\langle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}:\alpha<\delta\rangle
is a sequence of posets and
\vec{\pi}=\langle\pi_{\beta\alpha}:\alpha<\beta<\delta\rangle
is a sequence such that
\pi_{\beta\alpha}:\mathbb{P}_{\beta}\to\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
is a projection. Suppose also that whenever
\alpha<\beta<\gamma<\delta
, then
\pi_{\gamma\alpha}=\pi_{\beta\alpha}\circ\pi_{\gamma\beta}
. Such a pair of sequences is called an
inverse system
, and
\delta
is referred to as the
length
of the system. We define the
inverse limit
of the system,
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
, to be the set of all sequences
\langle p_{i}:i<\delta\rangle
such that for
\alpha<\beta<\delta
,
p_{\alpha}=\pi_{\beta\alpha}(p_{\beta})
. The ordering on
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
is pointwise.

We also allow inverse systems on which the maps
\pi_{\beta\alpha}
are not necessarily defined at every point of
\mathbb{P}_{\beta}
, and the maps are not assumed to commute everywhere.
In this case, we take
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
, to be the set of all sequences
\langle p_{i}:i<\delta\rangle
such that for all
\alpha<\beta<\delta
,
p_{\beta}\in\dom\pi_{\beta\alpha}
and
p_{\alpha}=\pi_{\beta\alpha}(p_{\beta})
.

Given an inverse system like this, recursively define sets
E_{\alpha}^{\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}\rangle}\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
as follows. Let
E_{0}^{\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}\rangle}=\mathbb{P}_{0}
, and for
0<\beta
, let
E_{\beta}^{\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}\rangle}=\bigcap_{\alpha<\beta}\pi^{-1}_{\beta\alpha}[E_{\alpha}^{\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}\rangle}]
.
It is easy to check by induction that for
\alpha<\delta
,
p\in E_{\alpha}^{\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}\rangle}
if and only if for each finite sequence
\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{n}=\alpha
,
\pi_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{0}}\circ\dots\circ\pi_{\alpha_{n}\alpha_{n-1}}(p)
is defined.

Sometimes, for notational convenience, we will refer to a map
\pi_{\alpha\alpha}
in an inverse system, by which we mean the identity map.

Lemma 13
.

Suppose
\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}\rangle
is an inverse system of length
\delta
, each
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
is separative, and for all
\alpha<\delta
, the map
\vec{p}\mapsto\vec{p}(\alpha)
is a projection. Then
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
is separative.

Proof.

Suppose
\vec{p},\vec{q}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
and
\vec{p}\nleq\vec{q}
. Then for some
\alpha<\delta
,
\vec{p}(\alpha)\nleq\vec{q}(\alpha)
. There is
x\leq\vec{p}(\alpha)
such that
x\perp\vec{q}(\alpha)
, and by hypothesis, there is
\vec{r}\leq\vec{p}
such that
\vec{r}(\alpha)\leq x
. Clearly
\vec{r}\perp\vec{q}
.
∎

Definition 14
.

For a regular uncountable cardinal
\kappa
, a pair
\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}\rangle
is called a
\kappa
-good inverse system
when for some ordinal
\delta
,

1.

\vec{\mathbb{P}}=\langle\mathbb{P}_{i}:i<\delta\rangle
is a sequence of completely
\kappa
-closed posets.

2.

\vec{\pi}=\langle\pi_{ji}:i<j<\delta\rangle
is a sequence of maps such that for all
i<j<\delta
,
D_{ji}=\dom\pi_{ji}
is a dense
\kappa
-closed subset of
\mathbb{P}_{j}
, and
\pi_{ji}:D_{ji}\to\mathbb{P}_{i}
is a
\kappa
-continuous projection.

3.

For
i<j<k<\delta
,
\pi_{ki}=\pi_{ji}\circ\pi_{kj}
at all points for which these are defined.

Lemma 15
.

Suppose
\kappa
is a regular uncountable cardinal and
\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}\rangle
is a
\kappa
-good inverse system of length
\omega
. Then:

1.

\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
is completely
\kappa
-closed.

2.

For each
i<\omega
, the map
\vec{p}\mapsto\vec{p}(i)
is a
\kappa
-continuous projection from
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
to
\mathbb{P}_{i}
.

Proof.

To show the first claim, suppose
\delta<\kappa
and
\langle\vec{p}_{\alpha}:\alpha<\delta\rangle
is a descending sequence in
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
. Then for each
i<\omega
,
\langle\vec{p}_{\alpha}(i):\alpha<\delta\rangle
is a descending sequence with an infimum
p_{i}^{*}
. Let
\vec{p}_{\delta}=\langle p_{i}^{*}:i<\omega\rangle
. The continuity of each
\pi_{ji}
implies that
\vec{p}_{\delta}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
. If
\vec{q}
is any other lower bound to the sequence
\langle\vec{p}_{\alpha}:\alpha<\delta\rangle
, then we would have
\vec{q}(i)\leq p^{*}_{i}
for each
i
, so
\vec{p}_{\delta}=\inf_{\alpha}\vec{p}_{\alpha}
.

For the second claim, first note that
\kappa
-continuity follows from the above paragraph, since if
\delta<\kappa
and
\langle\vec{p}_{\alpha}:\alpha<\delta\rangle
is descending, then
\inf_{\alpha}\vec{p}_{\alpha}
is given by taking
\inf_{\alpha}\vec{p}_{\alpha}(i)
at each coordinate.
Now let
E_{i}=E_{i}^{\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}\rangle}
for
i<\omega.

By Lemmas
8
and
9
, each
E_{i}
is a
\kappa
-closed dense subset of
\mathbb{P}_{i}
.
To show that
\vec{p}\mapsto\vec{p}(n)
is a projection, suppose
\vec{p}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
and
r\leq\vec{p}(n)
. Let
q_{n}\in E_{n}
be below
r
. Using the fact that
\pi_{n+1,n}
is a projection, let
q_{n+1}\in E_{n+1}
be such that
q_{n+1}\leq\vec{p}(n+1)
and
\pi_{n+1,n}(q_{n+1})\leq q_{n}
.
Assume inductively that
n<j
and we have chosen
q_{i}\in E_{i}
for
n\leq i\leq j
such that

p(i)\geq q_{i}\geq\pi_{i+1,i}(q_{i+1})\geq\pi_{i+2,i}(q_{i+2})\geq\dots\geq\pi_{ji}(q_{j}).

Then pick
q_{j+1}\in E_{j+1}
such that
q_{j+1}\leq\vec{p}(j+1)
and
\pi_{j+1,j}(q_{j+1})\leq q_{j}
.
The induction carries forward, and after
\omega
steps, we have that for all
i<\omega

\langle\pi_{ji}(q_{j}):\max\{n,i\}<j<\omega\rangle
is a descending sequence in
E_{i}
with infimum
q_{i}^{*}
. If we define
\vec{q}=\langle q^{*}_{i}:i<\omega\rangle
, then
\vec{q}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
by the continuity of each
\pi_{ji}
,
\vec{q}\leq\vec{p}
, and
\vec{q}(n)=q^{*}_{n}\leq r
.
∎

To make a similar claim about inverse systems of longer lengths, we need to assume that they behave nicely at limit points.

Lemma 16
.

Assume the following:

1.

\kappa
is a regular uncountable cardinal and
\delta<\kappa
.

2.

\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}\rangle
is a
\kappa
-good inverse system of length
\delta
.

3.

For all limit
\gamma<\delta
, the map
\sigma_{\gamma}:E_{\gamma}^{\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}\rangle}\to\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}{\restriction}\gamma,\vec{\pi}{\restriction}\gamma)
defined by
\sigma_{\gamma}(p)=\langle\pi_{\gamma\alpha}(p):\alpha<\gamma\rangle
is a projection.

Then
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
is completely
\kappa
-closed, and for each
i<\delta
, the map
\vec{p}\mapsto\vec{p}(i)
is a
\kappa
-continuous projection from
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
to
\mathbb{P}_{i}
.

Proof.

Complete
\kappa
-closure and
\kappa
-continuity follow by the same argument as in the case
\delta=\omega
.

Let us show that the map
\vec{p}\mapsto\vec{p}(i)
is a projection. Suppose
\vec{p}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
,
\alpha<\delta
, and
r\leq\vec{p}(\alpha)
. Let
E_{i}=E_{i}^{\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}\rangle}
for
i<\delta
. Note that by Lemmas
8
and
9
, each
E_{i}
is a
\kappa
-closed dense subset of
\mathbb{P}_{i}
. Let
q_{\alpha}\in E_{\alpha}
be below
r
.

Assume inductively that
\alpha<\gamma<\delta
and we have chosen
q_{i}\in E_{i}
for
\alpha\leq i<\gamma
such that
q_{i}\leq\vec{p}(i)
, and for all
\beta<\gamma
,
\langle\pi_{i\beta}(q_{i}):\max\{\alpha,\beta\}\leq i<\gamma\rangle
is a decreasing sequence in
E_{\beta}
. If
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}+1
, pick
q_{\gamma}\in E_{\gamma}
such that
q_{\gamma}\leq\vec{p}(\gamma)
and
\pi_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}(q_{\gamma})\leq q_{\gamma^{\prime}}
.

If
\gamma
is a limit, first define a
\gamma
-sequence
\vec{r}_{\gamma}
by

\vec{r}_{\gamma}(\beta)=\inf\{\pi_{i\beta}(q_{i}):\max\{\alpha,\beta\}\leq i<\gamma\rangle\}\in E_{\beta}.

Using
\kappa
-continuity, it is easy to check that
\vec{r}_{\gamma}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}{\restriction}\gamma,\vec{\pi}{\restriction}\gamma)
and
\vec{r}_{\gamma}\leq\vec{p}\restriction\gamma
. Then use our extra assumption to find
q_{\gamma}\in E_{\gamma}
such that
\sigma_{\gamma}(q_{\gamma})\leq\vec{r}_{\gamma}
. This allows the induction to continue. In the end, define
\vec{q}\in\prod_{i}E_{i}
by
\vec{q}(i)=\inf\{\pi_{ji}(q_{j}):\max\{\alpha,i\}\leq j<\delta\rangle\}
. Just as before, we have that
\vec{q}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
,
\vec{q}\leq\vec{p}
, and
\vec{q}(\alpha)\leq r
.
∎

Lemma 17
.

Under the same hypotheses as in Lemma
16
, assume additionally that for each
\alpha<\delta
, we are given a dense
\kappa
-closed
D_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
. Then
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})\cap\prod_{\alpha}D_{\alpha}
is a dense
\kappa
-closed subset of
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
.

Proof.

For
i<j<\delta
, replace
\pi_{ji}
with
\pi^{\prime}_{ji}=\pi_{ji}\restriction D_{j}
. It is easy to see that

\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})\cap\prod_{\alpha}D_{\alpha}=\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}^{\prime}).

\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}^{\prime})
is completely
\kappa
-closed by Lemma
16
, and since glb’s are computed pointwise,
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}^{\prime})
is a
\kappa
-closed subset of
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
.

To show that
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}^{\prime})
is dense in
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
, let
\vec{p}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
.
Let
E_{0}=D_{0}
, and
for
0<\alpha<\delta
, define
E_{\alpha}=E^{\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}^{\prime}\rangle}_{\alpha}
.
Let
q_{0}\leq\vec{p}(0)
be in
E_{0}
.

Assume inductively that
\gamma<\delta
and we have chosen
q_{i}\in E_{i}
for
i<\gamma
such that
q_{i}\leq\vec{p}(i)
, and
\langle\pi_{ji}(q_{j}):i\leq j<\gamma\rangle
is a decreasing sequence in
E_{i}
. If
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}+1
, pick
q_{\gamma}\in E_{\gamma}
such that
q_{\gamma}\leq\vec{p}(\gamma)
and
\pi_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}(q_{\gamma})\leq q_{\gamma^{\prime}}
.
If
\gamma
is a limit, first define
\vec{r}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}{\restriction}\gamma,\vec{\pi}{\restriction}\gamma)

by
\vec{r}(i)=\inf\{\pi_{ji}(q_{j}):i\leq j<\gamma\rangle\}\in E_{i}
.
We have that
\vec{r}\leq\vec{p}\restriction\gamma
.
Then find
q_{\gamma}\in E_{\gamma}
below
\vec{p}(\gamma)
such that
\sigma_{\gamma}(q_{\gamma})\leq\vec{r}
.

After
\delta
-many steps, define
q_{i}^{*}=\inf\{\pi_{ji}(q_{j}):i\leq j<\delta\rangle\}\in E_{i}
, and let
\vec{q}=\langle q_{i}^{*}:i<\delta\rangle
. We have that
\vec{q}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}^{\prime})
and
\vec{q}\leq\vec{p}
in
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
.
∎

Lemma 18
.

Assume the following:

1.

\kappa
is a regular uncountable cardinal and
\delta<\kappa
.

2.

\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}\rangle
is a
\kappa
-good inverse system of length
\delta
.

3.

X\subseteq\delta
is cofinal.

4.

For all
\gamma\in X
, the map
\sigma_{\gamma}:E_{\gamma}^{\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}}{\restriction}X,\vec{\pi}{\restriction}X\rangle}\to\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}\restriction(X\cap\gamma),\vec{\pi}\restriction(X\cap\gamma))
defined by
\sigma_{\gamma}(p)=\langle\pi_{\gamma\alpha}(p):\alpha\in X\cap\gamma\rangle
is a projection.

Then the map
\vec{p}\mapsto\vec{p}\restriction X
is a
\kappa
-continuous isomorphism of
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
with a dense
\kappa
-closed subset of
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}\restriction X,\vec{\pi}\restriction X)
.

Proof.

The map
\vec{p}\mapsto\vec{p}\restriction X
is clearly order-preserving.
It is injective since if
\vec{p}_{0}\not=\vec{p}_{1}
, then
\vec{p}_{0}(\alpha)\not=\vec{p}_{1}(\alpha)
for some
\alpha<\delta
, and thus
\vec{p}_{0}(\beta)\not=\vec{p}_{1}(\beta)
for all
\beta\in[\alpha,\delta)
, and hence
\vec{p}_{0}\restriction X\not=\vec{p}_{1}\restriction X
since
X
is cofinal. If
\vec{p}_{0}\restriction X\leq\vec{p}_{1}\restriction X
, then
\vec{p}_{0}\leq\vec{p}_{1}
since
X
is cofinal and each
\pi_{ji}
is order-preserving, so the map is an order-isomorphism with its range. The map is clearly
\kappa
-continuous since glb’s are computed pointwise.

To show that the range is dense, note that it is easy to see by induction that for all
\alpha<\delta
,
E_{\alpha}=E_{\alpha}^{\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}\rangle}
is a
\kappa
-closed dense subset of
E_{\alpha}^{\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}}\restriction X,\vec{\pi}\restriction X\rangle}
. By Lemma
17
,
E^{*}=\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}{\restriction}X,\vec{\pi}{\restriction}X)\cap\prod_{\alpha\in X}E_{\alpha}
is a
\kappa
-closed dense subset of
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}{\restriction}X,\vec{\pi}{\restriction}X)
. We claim that the map
\vec{p}\mapsto\vec{p}\restriction X
is a surjection from
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
to
E^{*}
.
For
\vec{q}\in E^{*}
, define
\vec{p}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi})
by
\vec{p}(\alpha)=\vec{q}(\alpha)
for
\alpha\in X
and for
\alpha\in\delta\setminus X
,
\vec{p}(\alpha)=\pi_{\beta\alpha}(\vec{q}(\beta))
for
\beta=\min(X\setminus\alpha)
. We have
\vec{p}(\alpha)\in E_{\alpha}
since
\pi_{\beta\alpha}[E_{\beta}]\subseteq E_{\alpha}
.
∎

Proposition 19
.

Assume the following:

1.

\kappa
is a regular uncountable cardinal and
\delta
is an ordinal such that
\cf(\delta)<\kappa
.

2.

\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}},\vec{\pi}\rangle
is a
\kappa
-good inverse system of length
\delta
.

3.

For all
X\subseteq\delta
of size
<\cf(\delta)
with a maximum element
\gamma
, the map
\sigma_{\gamma}:E_{\gamma}^{\langle\vec{\mathbb{P}}{\restriction}X,\vec{\pi}{\restriction}X\rangle}\to\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}\restriction(X\cap\gamma),\vec{\pi}\restriction(X\cap\gamma))
defined by
\sigma_{\gamma}(p)=\langle\pi_{\gamma\alpha}(p):\alpha\in X\cap\gamma\rangle
is a projection.

Then for all cofinal
A,B\subseteq\delta
of size
<\kappa
,

\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}{\restriction}A,\vec{\pi}{\restriction}A)
and

\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}{\restriction}B,\vec{\pi}{\restriction}B)

have isomorphic
\kappa
-closed dense subsets.

Proof.

Let
A,B\subseteq\delta
be as hypothesized, and let
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
be cofinal and of order type
\cf(\delta)
. Let
C=A\cup B
. By Lemma
18
, the restriction maps to
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
are
\kappa
-continuous dense embeddings of
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}{\restriction}C,\vec{\pi}{\restriction}C)
into
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}{\restriction}A^{\prime},\vec{\pi}{\restriction}A^{\prime})
and
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}{\restriction}B^{\prime},\vec{\pi}{\restriction}B^{\prime})
respectively. The restriction maps to
A
and
B
are also
\kappa
-continuous dense embeddings, because for example if
\vec{p}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}{\restriction}A,\vec{\pi}{\restriction}A)
, then
\vec{p}\restriction A^{\prime}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}{\restriction}A^{\prime},\vec{\pi}{\restriction}A^{\prime})
, and there is
\vec{q}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}{\restriction}C,\vec{\pi}{\restriction}C)
such that
\vec{q}\restriction A^{\prime}\leq\vec{p}\restriction A^{\prime}
. Hence
\vec{q}\restriction A\leq\vec{p}
, since
A^{\prime}
is cofinal in
A
.
∎

2.3
Reasonable collapses

Proposition 20
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}
are separative posets,
\mathbb{P}
is a regular suborder of
\mathbb{Q}
, and
\pi:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{P}
is a projection such that
\pi\restriction\mathbb{P}=\mathrm{id}
. Then for all
q\in\mathbb{Q}
,
q\leq\pi(q)
, and for all
p\in\mathbb{P}
and
q\in\mathbb{Q}
,
p\perp q
if and only if
p\perp\pi(q)
.

Proof.

For
q\in\mathbb{Q}
,
q\Vdash\pi(q)\in\pi(\dot{G})=\dot{G}\cap\mathbb{P}
, so by separativity,
q\leq\pi(q)
.

Suppose
p\in\mathbb{P}
and
q\in\mathbb{Q}
. If
r\leq p,q
, then
\pi(r)\leq p,\pi(q)
since
\pi(p)=p
. If
r\leq p,\pi(q)
, then there is
q^{\prime}\leq q
such that
\pi(q^{\prime})\leq r
, and then by the first claim,
q^{\prime}\leq\pi(q^{\prime})\leq p
, so
q^{\prime}\leq p,q
.
∎

The following is meant to abstract some general features of
\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)
and similar collapsing posets.

Definition 21
.

Suppose
\kappa
is regular and
\lambda>\kappa
is inaccessible. A poset
\mathbb{P}
is called a
reasonable
(\kappa,\lambda)
-collapse
when:

1.

\mathbb{P}
is
\lambda
-c.c. and completely
\kappa
-closed.

2.

There is a
\subseteq
-increasing sequence of regular suborders
\langle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\rangle
.

3.

There is a sequence of maps
\langle\pi_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\rangle
such that for all
\alpha
,
\pi_{\alpha}:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
is a
\kappa
-continuous projection,
\pi_{\alpha}\restriction\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}=\mathrm{id}
, and for
\alpha<\beta
,
\pi_{\alpha}=\pi_{\alpha}\circ\pi_{\beta}
.

4.

For unboundedly many regular
\alpha<\lambda
,
|\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}|=\alpha
and
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}}|\alpha|=\kappa
.

Definition 22
.

A poset
\mathbb{P}
is called a
quite reasonable

(\kappa,\lambda)
-collapse when there are
\langle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\rangle
and
\langle\pi_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\rangle
witnessing that
\mathbb{P}
is reasonable, with the following additional property:

Whenever
\cf(\alpha)<\kappa
, the map
p\mapsto\langle\pi_{\beta}(p):\beta<\alpha\rangle
is an isomorphism from
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
to
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}\restriction\alpha,\vec{\pi}\restriction\alpha)
, where
\vec{\pi}\restriction\alpha=\langle\pi_{\gamma}\restriction\mathbb{P}_{\beta}:\gamma<\beta<\alpha\rangle
.

Let us remark that the Levy, Silver, Laver, and Easton collapses are all quite reasonable.

3
Dual Shioya forcing

In this section, we will present our main building block.

In
[
27
]
, Shioya defined a forcing notion that generically glues together copies of the Levy collapse
\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\mu)
for
\mu<\lambda
, using complete embeddings with
\kappa
-closed quotients,
and showed how to use it in order to get dense ideals. Here we will use a variant of this forcing, using projections instead of embeddings. Those two definitions yield equivalent forcings,
5
5

5

Using Lemma
12
, one can obtain a complete embedding with a
\kappa
-closed quotient from a
\kappa
-continuous projection. On a dense subset of conditions
\langle\mathbb{P},p,\dot{\tau}\rangle
of
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
, such that
\mathbb{P}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\mu)
,
\mu
is a regular cardinal, and
\tau
codes the generic of
\mathbb{P}
, this modification is order-preserving from our poset to Shioya’s poset.
but using projections seems to be more natural with respect to the uniformization forcing in Section
4
. We will not use the equivalence of those forcings in the proofs.

Definition 23
.

Let
\omega<\kappa<\lambda
be regular cardinals,
\lambda
inaccessible.
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
6
6

6

W
is the Japanese hiragana character representing the first syllable of the name, “Shioya”. We caution that the associated sound is also the reading of the Japanese word for “death”, so before Japanese audiences, it may be advisable to pronounce the full name.

consists of all triples of the form
\langle\mathbb{P},p,\tau\rangle
where:

1.

\mathbb{P}\in V_{\lambda}
is a separative completely
\kappa
-closed poset.

2.

p\in\mathbb{P}
.

3.

For some
\gamma<\lambda
,
\tau
is a
\mathbb{P}
-name for a function from
\gamma
to
2
.

Let us define the order of
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
. Let
\langle\mathbb{P}_{0},p_{0},\tau_{0}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{P}_{1},p_{1},\tau_{1}\rangle
if there is a
\kappa
-continuous projection
\pi
from a
\kappa
-closed dense subset of
\mathbb{P}_{0}
to
\mathbb{P}_{1}
such that
p_{0}\Vdash p_{1}\in\pi(\dot{G})
, where
\dot{G}
is the canonical name for the
\mathbb{P}_{0}
-generic filter, and
p_{0}\Vdash\pi^{*}(\tau_{1})\trianglelefteq\tau_{0}
, where
\pi^{*}
is the canonical translation of
\mathbb{P}_{1}
-names into
\mathbb{P}_{0}
-names via the projection
\pi
.

Lemma 24
.

The ordering on
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
is transitive.

Proof.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{P}_{0},p_{0},\tau_{0}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{P}_{1},p_{1},\tau_{1}\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{P}_{1},p_{1},\tau_{1}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{P}_{2},p_{2},\tau_{2}\rangle
.
Let
D_{0},D_{1}
and
\pi_{0}
,
\pi_{1}
be such that for
i=0,1
,
D_{i}
is a
\kappa
-closed dense subset of
\mathbb{P}_{i}
, and
\pi_{i}:D_{i}\to\mathbb{P}_{i+1}
is a
\kappa
-continuous projection. By Lemma
10
, there is a
\kappa
-closed dense
E\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{0}
on which
\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{0}:E\to\mathbb{P}_{2}
is a
\kappa
-continuous projection. Let
\sigma=\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{0}
.

The
\sigma
-image of a generic
G_{0}
for
\mathbb{P}_{0}
induces a generic
G_{2}
for
\mathbb{P}_{2}
, and this is the same as taking the filter
G_{1}
induced by the
\pi_{0}
-image of
G_{0}
and then taking the filter induced by the
\pi_{1}
-image of
G_{1}
. Since
p_{0}
forces that
p_{1}\in\dot{G}_{1}
and
p_{1}
forces that
p_{2}\in\dot{G}_{2}
,
p_{0}
forces that
p_{2}\in\dot{G}_{2}
. Since
p_{0}
forces that

\pi_{0}^{*}(\tau_{1})\trianglelefteq\tau_{0}

and
p_{1}
forces that

\pi_{1}^{*}(\tau_{2})\trianglelefteq\tau_{1}
,
it follows that
p_{0}
forces that

\sigma^{*}(\tau_{2})\trianglelefteq\tau_{0}
.
∎

The following lemma shows the universality of
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
. A similar property was shown by Shioya
[
27
]
, using a very similar proof. The same property holds for the Anonymous collapse of
[
7
]
.

Lemma 25
.

Suppose
\mathbb{R}
is a reasonable
(\kappa,\lambda)
-collapse. Then, there is a dense subset
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}*\dot{\mathrm{Add}}(\lambda)
and a projection
\psi:D\to\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
.

Proof.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{R}_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\rangle
and
\langle\rho_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\rangle
witness that
\mathbb{R}
is a reasonable
(\kappa,\lambda)
-collapse. We can take
\mathbb{R}_{0}
to be
\{1_{\mathbb{R}}\}
.
Let
D
be the set of
\langle r,\dot{s}\rangle
such that for some
\alpha<\lambda
,
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\alpha}
,
\dot{s}
is a
\mathbb{R}_{\alpha}
-name, and
\dot{s}
is forced (by
1_{\mathbb{R}}
) to have domain
\alpha
. Using the
\lambda
-c.c., it is easy to see that
D
is dense. For
\langle r,\dot{s}\rangle\in D
with (unique) witnessing ordinal
\alpha
, let us call
\alpha
the
length
of
\langle r,\dot{s}\rangle
. For
\langle r,\dot{s}\rangle\in D
of length
\alpha
, let
\psi(r,\dot{s})=\langle\mathbb{R}_{\alpha},r,\dot{s}\rangle
.
\psi
is clearly order-preserving, with the maps
\rho_{\alpha}\restriction\mathbb{R}_{\beta}
for
\alpha\leq\beta
witnessing the order relations in
\ran\psi
. Note that
\psi(1_{\mathbb{R}},\check{\emptyset})=\langle\{1\},1,\check{\emptyset}\rangle
is a maximal element of
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
.

Suppose
\langle r_{0},\dot{s}_{0}\rangle\in D
has length
\alpha_{0}
and
\langle\mathbb{P},\bar{p},\tau\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{0}},r_{0},\dot{s}_{0}\rangle
. Let
\pi_{0}:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{0}}
be a
\kappa
-continuous projection defined on a dense
\kappa
-closed subset of
\mathbb{P}
witnessing the ordering. Strengthening the condition if necessary, we may assume
\bar{p}\in\dom\pi_{0}
. Let
\alpha_{1}
be a regular cardinal such that
\alpha_{0}\leq\alpha_{1}<\lambda
,
|\mathbb{P}|<\alpha_{1}
,
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\dom\tau<\check{\alpha}_{1}
,
|\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{1}}|=\alpha_{1}
and
\Vdash_{\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{1}}}|\alpha_{1}|=\kappa
. Extend
\langle\mathbb{P},\bar{p},\tau\rangle
to
\langle\mathbb{Q},\bar{q},\sigma\rangle
, where
\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{P}\times\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\alpha_{1})
,
\bar{q}=\langle\bar{p},1\rangle
, and
\sigma
is forced to be an end-extension of
\mathrm{pr}_{0}^{*}(\tau)
of length
\alpha_{1}
, where
\mathrm{pr}_{0}
is the projection
\langle a,b\rangle\mapsto a
.

For notational convenience, let
\mathbb{Q}_{0}=\mathbb{Q}
, let
\mathbb{Q}_{1}=\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{1}}
, let
\chi_{0}=\pi_{0}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{0}
, and let
\chi_{1}=\rho_{\alpha_{0}}\restriction\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{1}}
.

Claim 26
.

There are
e_{0},e_{1}
such that on some
\kappa
-closed dense sets, the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{1}=\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{0}}\times\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\alpha_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{0}=\mathbb{P}\times\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\alpha_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}

Namely, for
n<2
, there is a
\kappa
-closed dense
E_{n}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{0}}\times\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\alpha_{1})
and a
\kappa
-continuous dense embedding
e_{n}:E_{n}\to\mathbb{Q}_{n}
, with the property that if
\langle r,c\rangle\in E_{n}
, then
\chi_{n}(e_{n}(r,c))=r
.

The claim follows from Lemma
12
.

Now let
E=E_{0}\cap E_{1}
, which is
\kappa
-closed and dense. For
n<2
, let
F_{n}=e_{n}[E]
. There is a
\kappa
-continuous isomorphism
\iota:F_{1}\to F_{0}
defined by
\iota=e_{0}\circ e_{1}^{-1}
.
\mathrm{pr}_{0}\circ\iota
is a
\kappa
-continuous projection from
F_{1}
to
\mathbb{P}
.
If
q\in F_{1}
and
q=e_{1}(r,c)
, then

\pi_{0}\circ\mathrm{pr_{0}}\circ\iota(q)=\chi_{0}\circ e_{0}(r,c)=r=\chi_{1}\circ e_{1}(r,c)=\chi_{1}(q)=\rho_{\alpha_{0}}(q).

Take
r_{1}\in F_{1}
such that
\iota(r_{1})\leq\bar{q}
.
Let
\dot{s}_{1}
be any
\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{1}}
-name such that
r_{1}\Vdash\dot{s}_{1}=\iota^{*}(\sigma)
. Then we have that
\langle\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{1}},r_{1},\dot{s}_{1}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{P},\bar{p},\tau\rangle
, as witnessed by the projection
\mathrm{pr}_{0}\circ\iota
defined on the
\kappa
-closed dense set
F_{1}
.
Furthermore,
\langle r_{1},\dot{s}_{1}\rangle\leq\langle r_{0},\dot{s}_{0}\rangle
in
\mathbb{R}*\dot{\mathrm{Add}}(\lambda)
since
r_{1}\leq\rho_{\alpha_{0}}(r_{1})=\pi_{0}\circ\mathrm{pr_{0}}\circ\iota(r_{1})\leq\pi_{0}(\bar{p})\leq r_{0}
, and

r_{1}\Vdash\dot{s}_{1}\trianglerighteq(\mathrm{pr}_{0}\circ\iota)^{*}(\tau)\trianglerighteq(\pi_{0}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{0}\circ\iota)^{*}(\dot{s}_{0})=\rho_{\alpha_{0}}^{*}(\dot{s}_{0})=\dot{s}_{0}.\qed

Remark 27
.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{R}_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\rangle
and
\langle\rho_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\rangle
witness that
\mathbb{R}
is a reasonable
(\kappa,\lambda)
-collapse. Let
\psi:D\to\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
be as in the above lemma. For
\langle r,\dot{s}\rangle\in D
,
\psi(r,\dot{s})=\langle\mathbb{R}_{\alpha},r,\dot{s}\rangle
, where
\alpha
is the length of
\langle r,\dot{s}\rangle
.

It follows from the construction in the above lemma that
whenever
\langle r_{0},\dot{s}_{0}\rangle\in D
has length
\alpha_{0}
,
\langle\mathbb{P},p,\tau\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{0}},r_{0},\dot{s}_{0}\rangle
, and
\pi_{0}:\mathbb{P}\to\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{0}}
is a projection defined on a dense
\kappa
-closed set witnessing the ordering, then there is
\langle r_{1},\dot{s}_{1}\rangle\in D
of length
\alpha_{1}\geq\alpha_{0}
such that:

•

r_{1}\leq r_{0}
.

•

1_{\mathbb{R}}\Vdash\dot{s}_{1}\leq\dot{s}_{0}
.

•

\langle\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{1}},r_{1},\dot{s}_{1}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{P},p,\tau\rangle
.

•

If
r_{0}=1_{\mathbb{R}}
and
p=1_{\mathbb{P}}
, then we can take
r_{1}=1_{\mathbb{R}}
.

•

There is a projection
\pi_{1}:\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{1}}\to\mathbb{P}
defined on a dense
\kappa
-closed set witnessing the ordering such that
\pi_{0}\circ\pi_{1}=\rho_{\alpha_{0}}\restriction\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{1}}
.

Lemma 28
.

Let
G\subseteq\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
be generic,
\mathbb{R}
be a reasonable
(\kappa,\lambda)
-collapse, and
(\mathbb{R}*\dot{\mathrm{Add}}(\lambda))/G
be a quotient forcing via the projection
\psi
given by Lemma
25
.

Then,
(\mathbb{R}*\dot{\mathrm{Add}}(\lambda))/G
is
\lambda
-distributive in
V[G]
.

Proof.

Let
\langle\mathbb{R}_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{R}
witness that
\mathbb{R}
is reasonable.
Let
H*K\subseteq\mathbb{R}*\dot{\mathrm{Add}}(\lambda)
be generic such that
\psi(H{*}K)=G
. Let
k:\lambda\to 2
be the function corresponding to
K
.

First, we claim that
k\in V[G]
. Let
\alpha<\lambda
and let
\langle\mathbb{P}_{0},p_{0},\tau_{0}\rangle,\langle\mathbb{P}_{1},p_{1},\tau_{1}\rangle\in G
be such that
p_{0}\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}\tau(\alpha)=n_{0}
and
p_{1}\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}_{1}}\tau(\alpha)=n_{1}
. There is
\langle\mathbb{Q},q,\sigma\rangle\in G
below both
\langle\mathbb{P}_{0},p_{0},\tau_{0}\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{P}_{1},p_{1},\tau_{1}\rangle
, and by the definition of the ordering,
q\Vdash\sigma(\alpha)=n_{0}=n_{1}
, so
n_{0}=n_{1}
. Thus
G
determines a function
g:\lambda\to 2
by
g(\alpha)=n
iff for some
\langle\mathbb{P},p,\tau\rangle\in G
,
p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\tau(\alpha)=n
. Now if
\langle r,\dot{s}\rangle\in H*K
is a condition of length
\beta
and
r\Vdash_{\mathbb{R}_{\beta}}\dot{s}(\alpha)=n
, then
\psi(r,\dot{s})=\langle\mathbb{R}_{\beta},r,\dot{s}\rangle\in G
. Thus
g=k
.

For any set of ordinals
x\in V[H{*}K]
of size
\leq\kappa
, there is some
\alpha<\lambda
such that
x\in V[H\cap\mathbb{R}_{\alpha}]
. In
V[G]
, the set
H\cap\mathbb{R}_{\alpha}
is coded by some
y\subseteq\kappa
, and by a density argument there will be some
\beta<\lambda
such that
y=\{\gamma<\kappa:k(\beta+\gamma)=1\}
. Thus
y=\{\gamma<\kappa:g(\beta+\gamma)=1\}\in V[G]
, and so
x\in V[G]
.
∎

In the previous lemma, for
\langle\mathbb{P},p,\tau\rangle\in G
, we could not necessarily identify a generic for
\mathbb{P}
in the generic extension. Namely, it is possible that there are incompatible
p_{0},p_{1}\in\mathbb{P}
such that
\langle\mathbb{P},p_{0},\tau\rangle,\langle\mathbb{P},p_{1},\tau\rangle\in G
. The ordering of
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
ensures that such incompatible conditions must still decide the same information on
\tau
. In the next definition we identify a dense subset of conditions in which such a phenomenon cannot occur.

Definition 29

(Coding conditions)
.

We say
\langle\mathbb{P},p,\tau\rangle\in\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
is a
coding condition
when
\tau
is forced to have domain
\delta<\lambda
, and whenever
G\subseteq\mathbb{P}
is generic,

•

V[G]=V[\tau^{G}]
;

•

in all generic extensions of
V[G]
,
G
is the unique filter
F
that is
\mathbb{P}
-generic over
V
such that
\tau^{F}=\tau^{G}
.

We say that
\langle\mathbb{P},p,\tau\rangle
is a
strong coding condition
when
\tau
is forced to have domain
\delta<\lambda
, and there is a dense
D\subseteq\mathbb{P}
, a set
X\subseteq\delta
, and a function
f:X\to D
such that for all
\alpha\in X
,

1_{\mathbb{P}}\Vdash f(\alpha)\in\dot{G}\leftrightarrow\tau(\alpha)=1.

Lemma 30
.

Strong coding conditions are coding.

Proof.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{P},p,\tau\rangle\in\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
is strong coding, and let
\delta<\lambda
,
D\subseteq\mathbb{P}
,
X\subseteq\delta
, and
f:X\to D
witness. Let
G\subseteq\mathbb{P}
be generic and let
W
be any generic extension of
V[G]
.
D\cap G
can be read off from
\tau^{G}
, so
V[G]=V[\tau^{G}]
. If
H\in W
is a different filter that is
\mathbb{P}
-generic over
V
, then there is
p\in D\cap G
such that
p\notin H
. There is
\alpha\in X
such that
\tau^{G}(\alpha)=1
and
\tau^{H}(\alpha)=0
.
∎

Note that
\langle\mathbb{P},p,\tau\rangle
being a coding or strong coding condition depends only on
\mathbb{P}
and
\tau
and not on
p
.
It is easy to see that strong coding conditions are dense in
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
.
We also note that the property of being a coding condition is relatively local; a standard Löwenheim-Skolem argument shows that
\langle\mathbb{P},p,\tau\rangle
is a coding condition iff this is true in
H_{\theta}
, where
\theta>2^{|\mathbb{P}|}
is regular and
\langle\mathbb{P},p,\tau\rangle\in H_{\theta}
.

Suppose
\langle\langle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha},1_{\alpha},\tau_{\alpha}\rangle:\alpha<\delta\rangle\subseteq\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
is a descending sequence of coding conditions.
Suppose
\langle\mathbb{P}_{\delta},1_{\delta},\tau_{\delta}\rangle
is a lower bound,
\tau_{\delta}
is forced to the the union of the
\tau_{\alpha}
, and
\mathbb{P}_{\delta}
is in some sense a “limit” of the
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
, in that there is a dense set
D\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\delta}
and there are projections
\pi_{\delta\alpha}:D\to\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
, such that whenever
G\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\delta}
is generic, then
G\cap D=\{p\in D:(\forall\alpha<\delta)\pi_{\delta\alpha}(p)\in\pi_{\delta\alpha}(G)\}
. Then
\langle\mathbb{P}_{\delta},1_{\delta},\tau_{\delta}\rangle
is also coding, since
G
can be recovered from
\langle\pi_{\delta\alpha}(G):\alpha<\delta\rangle
, which can be recovered from
\tau_{\delta}^{G}=\bigcup_{\alpha<\delta}\tau_{\alpha}^{\pi_{\delta\alpha}(G)}
, and if
G^{\prime}\not=G
is another
\mathbb{P}_{\delta}
-generic filter, then for some
\alpha<\delta
,
\pi_{\delta\alpha}(G)\not=\pi_{\delta\alpha}(G^{\prime})
, and thus
\tau_{\delta}^{G}\not=\tau_{\delta}^{G^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 31

(Projection freezing)
.

Let
\langle\mathbb{P},p,\tau\rangle
be a coding condition and let
\langle\mathbb{Q},q,\sigma\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{P},p,\tau\rangle
. For any dense
D\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
, if
\pi_{0},\pi_{1}
are projections from
D
to
\mathbb{P}
such that
q\Vdash\pi_{i}^{*}(\tau)\trianglelefteq\sigma
for
i=0,1
, then
\pi_{0}(r)=\pi_{1}(r)
for all
r\leq q
in
D
.

Proof.

Let
D,\pi_{0},\pi_{1}
be as hypothesized, and let
\delta<\lambda
be the forced domain of
\tau
.
Suppose
r\leq q
,
r\in D
, and
\pi_{0}(r)\not=\pi_{1}(r)
; let us assume
\pi_{0}(r)\nleq\pi_{1}(r)
. Let
p^{\prime}\leq\pi_{0}(r)
be such that
p^{\prime}\perp\pi_{1}(r)
, and let
r^{\prime}\leq r
be such that
\pi_{0}(r^{\prime})\leq p^{\prime}
. Thus we have
\pi_{0}(r^{\prime})\perp\pi_{1}(r^{\prime})
since
\pi_{1}(r^{\prime})\leq\pi_{1}(r)\perp p^{\prime}
.

Let
H\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
be generic with
r^{\prime}\in H
. Let
G_{0},G_{1}
be the
\mathbb{P}
-generics induced by
\pi_{0},\pi_{1}
respectively; note
G_{0}\not=G_{1}
. Since
q\in H
,
\tau^{G_{0}}=\tau^{G_{1}}=\sigma^{H}\restriction\delta
. But this is a contradiction since there is only one
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter
F\in V[H]
with
\tau^{F}=\sigma^{H}\restriction\delta
.
∎

Lemma 32
.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{P},1_{\mathbb{P}},\tau\rangle\in\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
is a coding condition, and let
G\subseteq\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
be generic over
V
with
\langle\mathbb{P},1_{\mathbb{P}},\tau\rangle\in G
. Then

G_{\mathbb{P}}^{\tau}=\{p\in\mathbb{P}:\langle\mathbb{P},p,\tau\rangle\in G\}

is a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
.

Proof.

First, let us check that that
G_{\mathbb{P}}^{\tau}
is a filter. Suppose
\langle\mathbb{P},p_{0},\tau\rangle,\langle\mathbb{P},p_{1},\tau\rangle\in G
. If
\langle\mathbb{Q},q,\sigma\rangle\in\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
is below both
\langle\mathbb{P},p_{0},\tau\rangle
and
\langle\mathbb{P},p_{1},\tau\rangle
, with projections
\pi_{0},\pi_{1}:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{P}
witnessing each order relation respectively, then we can find
q^{\prime}\leq q
such that
q^{\prime}\in\dom\pi_{0}\cap\dom\pi_{1}
.
Therefore there is a dense set of conditions
\langle\mathbb{Q},q,\sigma\rangle\in\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
that are either incompatible with one of
\langle\mathbb{P},p_{0},\tau\rangle,\langle\mathbb{P},p_{1},\tau\rangle
, or below both with
q
in the domain of each of a pair of projections witnessing the order relations. Since
\langle\mathbb{P},p_{0},\tau\rangle,\langle\mathbb{P},p_{1},\tau\rangle\in G
, the latter option appears in
G
. If
\langle\mathbb{Q},q,\sigma\rangle\in G
is below both, with witnessing projections
\pi_{0},\pi_{1}:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{P}
, with
q\in\dom\pi_{0}\cap\dom\pi_{1}
, then
\pi_{0}(q)=\pi_{1}(q)
by Lemma
31
, since
\langle\mathbb{P},1,\tau\rangle
is coding. Thus
\langle\mathbb{P},\pi_{0}(q),\tau\rangle\in G
and
\pi_{0}(q)\leq p_{0},p_{1}
.

To show that
G_{\mathbb{P}}^{\tau}
is generic, let
D\subseteq\mathbb{P}
be an open and dense set in
V
. If
\langle\mathbb{Q},q,\sigma\rangle\in\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
is below
\langle\mathbb{P},1,\tau\rangle
, then if
\pi:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{P}
is a witnessing projection, there is
q^{\prime}\leq q
such that
\pi(q^{\prime})\in D
. Therefore there is a dense set of conditions
\langle\mathbb{Q},q,\sigma\rangle
that are either incompatible with
\langle\mathbb{P},1,\tau\rangle
, or below it and such that
q\in\pi^{-1}[D]
for a witnessing projection
\pi
. If
\langle\mathbb{Q},q,\sigma\rangle\in G
is in this dense set, the the latter option must occur, so for some projection
\pi:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{P}
,
\pi(q)\in D\cap G_{\mathbb{P}}^{\tau}
.
∎

Remark 33
.

If
\langle\mathbb{P},1,\tau\rangle
is a coding condition, then there is a projection
\pi
from a dense subset of
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)\restriction\langle\mathbb{P},1,\tau\rangle
to
\mathbb{P}
. For any
\langle\mathbb{Q},q,\sigma\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{P},1,\tau\rangle
, if there is a projection
\rho
witnessing the ordering with
q\in\dom\rho
, let
\pi(\langle\mathbb{Q},q,\sigma\rangle)=\rho(q)
. This is well-defined because by Lemma
31
, any two
\rho_{0},\rho_{1}
with these properties must have
\rho_{0}(q)=\rho_{1}(q)
. It is easy to check that
\pi
is a projection.

Remark 34
.

\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
is never countably closed.

To see this, let
\mathbb{P}
be any nontrivial completely
\kappa
-closed separative poset in
V_{\lambda}
.
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{P})
contains a countable maximal antichain,
\langle a_{i}:i<\omega\rangle
. If
b_{i}=\sum_{j\geq i}a_{j}
, then
\langle b_{i}:i<\omega\rangle
is a descending sequence without a lower bound. Viewing
\mathbb{P}
as a dense subset of
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{P})
, let
\mathbb{P}_{i}=\mathbb{P}\cup\{b_{i}\}
for each
i<\omega
. Note that each
\mathbb{P}_{i}
is completely
\kappa
-closed, as the single extra point
b_{i}
does not affect this.

Now, let
\tau
be a name such that
\langle\mathbb{P},1,\tau\rangle
is a coding condition. The sequence
\langle\langle\mathbb{P}_{i},b_{i},\tau\rangle:i<\omega\rangle
is a descending sequence in
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
, with
\mathrm{id}\restriction\mathbb{P}
witnessing each order relation. A lower bound to this sequence would force that for each
i
,
G^{\tau}_{\mathbb{P}_{i}}
is a generic filter for
\mathbb{P}_{i}
possessing
b_{i}
. We must also have that
G^{\tau}_{\mathbb{P}_{i}}\restriction\mathbb{P}=G^{\tau}_{\mathbb{P}}
for all
i<\omega
. Thus, if
H
is the filter on
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{P})
generated by
G^{\tau}_{\mathbb{P}}
, then
H
contains the descending sequence
\langle b_{i}:i<\omega\rangle
. But this contradicts the fact that
H
must possess one of the
a_{i}
by genericity.

As a corollary of the Freezing Lemma, we conclude that (at least locally) the projections that witness the order of the conditions in
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
commute.

Lemma 35
.

Suppose
\langle\mathbb{P}_{0},p_{0},\tau_{0}\rangle,\langle\mathbb{P}_{1},p_{1},\tau_{1}\rangle,\langle\mathbb{P}_{2},p_{2},\tau_{2}\rangle
are elements of
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
such that:

1.

\langle\mathbb{P}_{2},p_{2},\tau_{2}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{P}_{1},p_{1},\tau_{1}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{P}_{0},p_{0},\tau_{0}\rangle
.

2.

\langle\mathbb{P}_{0},p_{0},\tau_{0}\rangle
is a coding condition.

3.

For
i<j
,
i,j\in\{0,1,2\}
,
\pi_{j,i}
is a projection witnessing
\langle\mathbb{P}_{j},p_{j},\tau_{j}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{P}_{i},p_{i},\tau_{i}\rangle
.

Then for densely many
q\leq p_{2}
,
\pi_{2,0}(q)=\pi_{1,0}\circ\pi_{2,1}(q)
.

In Lemmas
40
and
41
we will prove strengthenings of the following lemmas:

Lemma 36
.

\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
is
\kappa
-strategically closed.

Lemma 37
.

\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
forces
\lambda=\kappa^{+}
.

4
Uniformization

Before diving into the details, let us motivate our construction. In the standard constructions of saturated ideals, we lift an elementary embedding (typically, an almost-huge one) through a two-stage collapse
\mathbb{P}*\dot{\mathbb{R}}
. We start with an embedding
j\colon V\to M
such that
\crit(j)=\kappa
,
j(\kappa)=\lambda
, and
M^{<\lambda}\subseteq M
.
\mathbb{P}\subseteq V_{\kappa}
turns
\kappa
into a successor cardinal
\mu^{+}
, and
\mathbb{R}
is a reasonable
(\kappa,\lambda)
-collapse in
V^{\mathbb{P}}
.

Typically, we will get that
j(\mathbb{P})
projects to
\mathbb{P}*\dot{\mathbb{R}}
. So if
G^{\prime}\subseteq j(\mathbb{P})
is generic, then we obtain a generic
G*H\subseteq\mathbb{P}*\dot{\mathbb{R}}
, and we can lift the embedding first to
j\colon V[G]\to M[G^{\prime}]
. As any initial segment of
\mathbb{R}
is smaller than
\lambda
, the set
j[H{\restriction}\alpha]
belongs to
M[G^{\prime}]
for
\alpha<\lambda
, and using some closure property of
j(\mathbb{R})
, we obtain a lower bound
m_{\alpha}\in j(\mathbb{R})\restriction j(\alpha)
for this set.

In order to extend the
master sequence

\langle m_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\rangle
to an
M
-generic filter for
j(\mathbb{R})
, we must extend each
m_{\alpha}
in a way that will be compatible with later extensions of
m_{\beta}
,
\alpha<\beta
. In other words, we are using the fact that the
restriction map is a projection
. So, we can extend each
m_{\alpha}
to some condition in
j(\mathbb{R}\restriction\alpha)
meeting some antichain which is bounded by coordinate
j(\alpha)
, knowing that we will not obtain a disagreement with
m_{\beta}
for larger
\beta
.

At limit points
\delta
of this process, we must verify that
m_{\delta}
is compatible with the
sequence
of steps that we already made. This is not a consequence of the existence of projections from
j(\mathbb{R})\restriction\delta
to the lower parts. Instead, we need to verify that this projection
factors through the inverse limit
of the system of forcing notions and projections that we considered so far.
7
7

7

This is not automatic, even for very well-behaved forcing notions and projections. Indeed, let
\mathbb{Q}_{n}=\mathrm{Add}(\omega_{1},n)
, namely all partial functions from
\omega_{1}\times n
to
2
with countable domain. Let
\pi_{n,m}\colon\mathbb{Q}_{n}\to\mathbb{Q}_{m}
be the restriction map. Let
\mathbb{Q}_{\omega}
be the collection of all functions
f\colon\alpha\times\omega\to 2
, with
\alpha<\omega_{1}
, such that for all
\beta<\alpha
,
\{n<\omega:f(\beta,n)=1\}
is finite. Let
\pi_{\omega,n}:\mathbb{Q}_{\omega}\to\mathbb{Q}_{n}
be the restriction map
f\mapsto f\restriction(\omega_{1}\times n)
. The system
\langle\pi_{\beta,\alpha}:\alpha<\beta\leq\omega\rangle
is a commuting system of continuous projections, but
\mathbb{Q}_{\omega}
is not the inverse limit of the system
\langle\mathbb{Q}_{n},\pi_{n,m}:m<n<\omega\rangle
. It is not difficult to arrange that
\mathbb{Q}_{\omega}
appears in a generic
G\subseteq\text{\char 87\relax}(\omega_{1},\lambda)
, as well as
\mathbb{Q}_{n}
for all
n
, with the above projections witnessing the ordering of their conditions, by picking the corresponding names
\tau
in a suitable way. In this case, no other forcing that appears in
G
can factor through the inverse limit of
\langle\mathbb{Q}_{n},\pi_{n,m}:n<m<\omega\rangle
.

In our forcing, we are using the Dual Shioya collapse
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
after some first stage
\mathbb{P}\subseteq V_{\kappa}
. Suppose that in some forcing extension, we have a lifted embedding
j\colon V[G]\to M[G^{\prime}]
, where
G,G^{\prime}
are generic for
\mathbb{P},j(\mathbb{P})
respectively. Suppose we also have a generic
H\subseteq\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)^{V[G]}
such that
V_{\alpha}\cap H\in M[G^{\prime}]
for all
\alpha<\lambda
. In order to be able to lift the embedding through
V[G][H]
, we need to add structure to
H
. We isolate a sequence of forcing notions and (almost) commuting projections between them. Moreover, we want the projections to respect small inverse limits.

This can be done by adding the quotient relative to
\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)*\mathrm{Add}(\lambda)
, but this would be too aggressive. We want to maintain the universality of the forcing notion, in the sense of Lemma
53
. So, we add a more complicated, but weaker structure.

Definition 38
.

Let
\mathbb{U}(G)
be the set of triples
u=\langle\vec{\mathbb{Q}}^{u},\vec{\pi}^{u},\vec{q}^{u}\rangle
such that:

1.

For some
\delta<\lambda
,
\vec{\mathbb{Q}}^{u}=\langle\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\alpha},1_{\alpha},\tau_{\alpha}\rangle:\alpha\leq\delta\rangle
,
\vec{\pi}^{u}=\langle\pi_{\beta\alpha}:\alpha<\beta\leq\delta\rangle
, and
\vec{q}^{u}=\langle q_{\beta\alpha}:\alpha<\beta\leq\delta\rangle
.

2.

For each
\alpha\leq\delta
,
\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\alpha},1_{\alpha},\tau_{\alpha}\rangle\in G
and
\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\alpha},1_{\alpha},\tau_{\alpha}\rangle
is a coding condition.

3.

For each
\alpha<\beta\leq\delta
,
\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\beta},q_{\beta\alpha},\tau_{\beta}\rangle\in G
, and
\pi_{\beta\alpha}
is a
\kappa
-continuous projection from a
\kappa
-closed dense subset of
\mathbb{Q}_{\beta}
to
\mathbb{Q}_{\alpha}
that witnesses the order relation
\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\beta},q_{\beta\alpha},\tau_{\beta}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\alpha},1_{\alpha},\tau_{\alpha}\rangle
. We denote
\pi_{\beta\beta}:=id_{\mathbb{Q}_{\beta}}
.

4.

Suppose
x\in[\delta+1]^{<\kappa}
has a top element
\gamma
. If
q\in\mathbb{Q}_{\gamma}
is such that
q\in E_{\gamma}^{\langle\vec{\mathbb{Q}}\restriction x,\vec{\pi}\restriction x\rangle}

and
\pi_{\gamma\beta}(q)\leq q_{\beta\alpha}
for all
\{\alpha,\beta\}\in[x]^{2}
, then below
q
, the natural map into the inverse limit along
x\cap\gamma
is a projection.
8
8

8

Recall that
E_{\gamma}^{\langle\vec{\mathbb{Q}}\restriction x,\vec{\pi}\restriction x\rangle}
is the set of all
p\in\mathbb{Q}_{\gamma}
such that for all finite sequences
\alpha_{0}<\alpha_{1}<\dots<\alpha_{n}=\gamma
contained in
x
,
\pi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{0}}\circ\dots\circ\pi_{\alpha_{n},\alpha_{n-1}}
is defined at
p
. It is a
\kappa
-closed dense set.

More specifically, for all such
q
and all

\vec{r}\in\varprojlim\left(\vec{\mathbb{Q}}\restriction(x\cap\gamma),\vec{\pi}\restriction(x\cap\gamma)\right)

pointwise below
\langle\pi_{\gamma\alpha}(q):\alpha\in x\cap\gamma\rangle
, there is
q^{\prime}\leq q
such that

\langle\pi_{\gamma\alpha}(q^{\prime}):\alpha\in x\cap\gamma\rangle\leq\vec{r}.

We define
u^{\prime}=\langle\vec{\mathbb{Q}}^{u^{\prime}},\pi^{u^{\prime}},\vec{q}^{u^{\prime}}\rangle\leq u=\langle\vec{\mathbb{Q}}^{u},\pi^{u},\vec{q}^{u}\rangle
when
\vec{\mathbb{Q}}^{u^{\prime}}\supseteq\vec{\mathbb{Q}}^{u}
,
\vec{\pi}^{u^{\prime}}\supseteq\vec{\pi}^{u}
, and
{\vec{q}\ }^{u^{\prime}}\supseteq{\vec{q}\ }^{u}
.
9
9

9

We will omit the superscript
u
when it is clear from the context, and add it to the components of the forcing for emphasis, when needed.

One way to think about the forcing
\mathbb{U}(G)
is as a threading forcing, similar to the forcing adding a thread to an anti-compactness principle, as in
[
5
]
and many subsequent works. The forcing
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
adds a collection of collapsing generics connected via unspecified projections. In a larger generic extension (for example, in the generic extension by
\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)*\mathrm{Add}(\lambda)
), one can find those projections explicitly. Since the quotient map between
\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,{<}\lambda)\ast\mathrm{Add}(\lambda)
and
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
is
\lambda
-distributive, we expect the forcing
\mathbb{U}(G)
to be
\lambda
-distributive as well.

Let us remark on the connection between (
3
) and (
4
). Suppose
\langle\vec{\mathbb{Q}},\vec{\pi},\vec{q}\rangle
is a condition of length
\delta+1
. Suppose
\alpha<\beta<\gamma\leq\delta
, and
q\in\mathbb{Q}_{\gamma}
is such that the maps
\pi_{\gamma\alpha}
and
\pi_{\beta\alpha}\circ\pi_{\gamma\beta}
are defined at
q
,
q\leq q_{\gamma\beta},q_{\gamma\alpha}
, and
\pi_{\gamma\beta}(q)\leq q_{\beta\alpha}
.
Then
\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\gamma},q,\tau_{\gamma}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\alpha},1_{\alpha},\tau_{\alpha}\rangle
, and the maps
\pi_{\gamma\alpha}
and
\pi_{\beta\alpha}\circ\pi_{\gamma\beta}
both witness this relation. Therefore, since
\langle\mathbb{Q}_{\alpha},1_{\alpha},\tau_{\alpha}\rangle
is a coding condition, Lemma
31
implies that
\pi_{\gamma\alpha}(q)=\pi_{\beta\alpha}\circ\pi_{\gamma\beta}(q)
.

Thus if
x,\gamma,q
satisfy the hypotheses of (
4
), then
