\langle\dot{\mathbb{P}},\dot{1},\dot{\tau}\rangle^{G*U}=\langle\mathbb{P},1,\tau\rangle
. Let
\psi(1,\langle\dot{\mathbb{P}},\dot{1},\dot{\tau}\rangle)=\langle\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\kappa)*\dot{\mathbb{P}},\langle 1,\dot{1}\rangle,\sigma\rangle
. Then we have that
(G^{\prime})^{\sigma}_{\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\kappa)*\dot{\mathbb{P}}}=B*H^{\tau}_{\mathbb{P}}
. Thus
x\in V[G^{\prime}]
.
∎

Lemma 53
.

Suppose
\mu<\kappa<\lambda
are regular cardinals, with
\kappa,\lambda
inaccessible. Suppose

G_{0}*U_{0}*G_{1}*U_{1}\subseteq\text{\char 87\relax}(\mu,\kappa)*\dot{\mathbb{U}}(\dot{G}_{0})*\dot{\text{\char 87\relax}}(\kappa,\lambda)*\dot{\mathbb{U}}(\dot{G}_{1})
is generic over
V
, and
K\subseteq\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\kappa)
is generic over
V[G_{0}{*}U_{0}{*}G_{1}{*}U_{1}]
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be the tree forcing of Lemma
43
, and let
B\subseteq\mathbb{T}
be the generic corresponding to
G_{0}*U_{0}*K
. Let
\psi(B{*}G_{1})=G_{2}\subseteq\text{\char 87\relax}(\mu,\lambda)
, where
\psi
is the projection of Lemma
49
.

Then
\mathbb{U}(G_{2})
is
\lambda
-strategically closed in
V[B{*}G_{1}{*}U_{1}]
.

Proof.

Let
\langle\langle\mathbb{R}_{i},1,\varsigma_{i}\rangle:i<\lambda\rangle
,
\langle\rho_{ji}:i<j<\lambda\rangle
and
\langle r_{ji}:i<j<\lambda\rangle
be the sequences given by
U_{1}
.
Since double coding conditions are dense in
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
, the argument of Lemma
39
shows that cofinally many
\langle\mathbb{R}_{i},1,\varsigma_{i}\rangle
are double coding. Passing to a subsequence, let us assume that all
\langle\mathbb{R}_{i},1,\varsigma_{i}\rangle
are double coding.
For each
i<j<\lambda
, choose
(\text{\char 87\relax}(\mu,\kappa)*\dot{\mathbb{U}}(\dot{G}_{0}))
-names for these objects that evalutate to them:

\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{i}
,
\dot{\varsigma}_{i}
,
\dot{\rho}_{ji}
,
\dot{r}_{ji}
.
We may assume there is an increasing sequence of cardinals
\langle\delta_{i}:i<\lambda\rangle
such that
\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{i}
is forced to be equivalent to
\dot{\mathrm{Col}}(\check{\kappa},\check{\delta}_{i})
.
We may choose the names such that
\langle\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{i},1,\dot{\varsigma}_{i}\rangle
is forced to be double coding and
\dot{\rho}_{ji}
is forced to be a projection from
\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{j}
to
\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{i}
defined on a
\kappa
-closed dense set.
For every
i<j
, some condition in
G_{0}*U_{0}
will force that
\dot{\rho}_{ji}
witnesses
\langle\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{j},\dot{r}_{ji},\dot{\varsigma}_{j}\rangle\leq\langle\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{i},1,\dot{\varsigma}_{i}\rangle
.

Suppose that in
V[B{*}G_{1}{*}U_{1}]
, Players I and II are playing the game on
\mathbb{U}(G_{2})
.
Suppose Player I plays
u_{0}=\langle\langle\langle\mathbb{P}^{u_{0}}_{i},1,\tau_{i}^{u_{0}}\rangle:i\leq\xi_{0}\rangle,\langle\pi^{u_{0}}_{ji}:i<j\leq\xi_{0}\rangle,\langle q^{u_{0}}_{ji}:i<j\leq\xi_{0}\rangle\rangle
.
The argument of Lemma
39
shows that
there is
\alpha_{1}<\lambda
such that Player II can respond with a condition
u_{1}
of length
\xi_{0}+2
with top
\text{\char 87\relax}(\mu,\lambda)
-condition
\langle\mathbb{P}_{\xi_{0}+1}^{u_{1}},1,\tau_{\xi_{0}+1}^{u_{1}}\rangle=\langle\mathbb{T}*\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{\alpha_{1}},1,\sigma_{1}\rangle
, where
\langle\mathbb{T}*\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{\alpha_{1}},1,\sigma_{1}\rangle=\psi(1,\langle\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{\alpha_{1}},1,\dot{\varsigma}_{\alpha_{1}}\rangle)
.

Suppose Player I responds with
u_{2}=\langle\langle\langle\mathbb{P}^{u_{2}}_{i},1,\tau^{u_{0}}_{i}\rangle:i\leq\xi_{2}\rangle,\langle\pi^{u_{2}}_{ji}:i<j\leq\xi_{2}\rangle,\langle q^{u_{2}}_{ji}:i<j\leq\xi_{2}\rangle\rangle
. There is an ordinal
\alpha_{3}<\lambda
and a condition
\langle t_{3},\dot{r}_{3}\rangle\in B*(G_{1})^{\varsigma_{\alpha_{3}}}_{\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{3}}}
such that
\alpha_{3}>\alpha_{1}
,

t_{3}\Vdash\dot{r}_{3}\leq\dot{r}_{\alpha_{3}\alpha_{1}}\wedge\langle\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{\alpha_{3}},\dot{r}_{3},\dot{\varsigma}_{\alpha_{3}}\rangle\leq\langle\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{\alpha_{1}},1,\dot{\varsigma}_{\alpha_{1}}\rangle
,
and
\psi(t_{3},\langle\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{\alpha_{3}},\dot{r}_{3},\dot{\varsigma}_{\alpha_{3}}\rangle)=\langle\mathbb{T}*\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{\alpha_{3}},\langle t_{3},\dot{r}_{3}\rangle,\sigma_{3}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{P}_{\xi_{2}},1,\tau_{\xi_{2}}\rangle
.

Let
\chi
be a projection witnessing
\langle\mathbb{T}*\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{\alpha_{3}},\langle t_{3},\dot{r}_{3}\rangle,\sigma_{3}\rangle\leq\langle\mathbb{P}_{\xi_{2}},1,\tau_{\xi_{2}}\rangle
.
Let Player II’s next move be
u_{3}
of length
\xi_{2}+2
such that:

•

\langle\mathbb{P}^{u_{3}}_{\xi_{2}+1},1,\tau^{u_{3}}_{\xi_{2}+1}\rangle=\langle\mathbb{T}*\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{\alpha_{3}},1,\sigma_{3}\rangle
.

•

\pi^{u_{3}}_{\xi_{2}+1,\beta}=\pi^{u_{2}}_{\xi_{2},\beta}\circ\chi
for
\xi_{0}+1<\beta\leq\xi_{2}
,
\pi^{u_{3}}_{\xi_{2}+1,\xi_{0}+1}=\mathrm{id}\times\dot{\rho}_{\alpha_{3}\alpha_{1}}
, and
\pi^{u_{3}}_{\xi_{2}+1,\beta}=\pi^{u_{2}}_{\xi_{0}+1,\beta}\circ(\mathrm{id}\times\dot{\rho}_{\alpha_{3}\alpha_{1}})
for
\beta\leq\xi_{0}
, restricted to the dense
\mu
-closed sets on which these are defined.

•

q_{\xi_{2}+1,\beta}^{u_{3}}\leq\langle t_{3},\dot{r}_{3}\rangle
projects below
q_{\xi_{2},\beta}^{u_{2}}
for
\xi_{0}+1<\beta\leq\xi_{2}
,
q_{\xi_{2}+1,\xi_{0}+1}^{u+3}=\langle t_{3},\dot{r}_{3}\rangle
, and
q^{u_{3}}_{\xi_{2}+1,\beta}\leq\langle t_{3},\dot{r}_{3}\rangle
projects below
q_{\xi_{0}+1,\beta}^{u_{2}}
for
\beta\leq\xi_{0}
.

The argument that this fulfills requirement (
4
) is the same as for Player II’s second move in Lemma
40
.

Assume inductively that
\delta<\lambda
, and the game has gone on for
\delta
-many rounds, with the following properties:

•

At step
\beta
, the move
u_{\beta}=\langle\langle\langle\mathbb{P}^{u_{\beta}}_{i},1,\tau^{u_{\beta}}_{i}\rangle:i\leq\xi_{\beta}\rangle,\langle\pi^{u_{\beta}}_{ji}:i<j\leq\xi_{\beta}\rangle,\langle q^{u_{\beta}}_{ji}:i<j\leq\xi_{\beta}\rangle\rangle
for
\beta<\delta
, with
Player II has played in odd finite rounds and even infinite rounds.
As those conditions are descending, we may drop the superscript
u_{\beta}
.

•

There is an increasing sequence of ordinals
\langle\alpha_{\beta}:\beta<\delta\rangle
such that for odd finite and even infinite
\beta
,
\langle\mathbb{P}_{\xi_{\beta}},1,\tau_{\xi_{\beta}}\rangle=\psi(1,\langle\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{\alpha_{\beta}},1,\dot{\varsigma}_{\alpha_{\beta}}\rangle)
.

•

For
\beta<\gamma
both rounds where II played,
\pi_{\xi_{\gamma}\xi_{\beta}}=\mathrm{id}\times\dot{\rho}_{\alpha_{\gamma}\alpha_{\beta}}
restricted to some
\mu
-closed dense set of conditions
\langle t,\dot{r}\rangle
such that
t\Vdash\dot{r}\in\dom\dot{\rho}_{\alpha_{\gamma}\alpha_{\beta}}
, and
q_{\gamma\beta}=\langle t,\dot{r}\rangle\in B*(G_{1})^{\varsigma_{\alpha_{\gamma}}}_{\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{\gamma}}}
such that

t\Vdash\dot{r}\leq\dot{r}_{\alpha_{\gamma}\alpha_{\beta}}\wedge\langle\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{\alpha_{\gamma}},\dot{r},\dot{\varsigma}_{\alpha_{\gamma}}\rangle\leq\langle\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{\alpha_{\beta}},1,\dot{\varsigma}_{\alpha_{\beta}}\rangle
.

Let
\delta=\bar{\delta}+1
be a successor ordinal and assume the last round was a move of Player I. Then, Player II responds just as in round 3.

If
\delta
is a limit, then
|\delta|^{V[B{*}G_{1}{*}U_{1}]}\leq\mu
.
Let
\xi_{\delta}=\sup_{\gamma<\delta}\xi_{\gamma}
and
\alpha^{\prime}_{\delta}=\sup_{\gamma<\delta}\alpha_{\gamma}
. Let
v\in U_{1}
be large enough so that
\dom v
is larger than
\alpha^{\prime}_{\delta}
.

Here we must address a delicate point that was silenced until now. The generic filter for
\mathbb{U}(\dot{G}_{1})
is composed of names which are forced by conditions in
G_{0}{*}U_{0}
to be compatible in
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
, as witnessed by the projections
\dot{\rho}_{ji}
. Those relations between pairs of those names are not forced outright but only by sufficiently strong conditions in the generic filter.
At the successor step, it is enough to let the corresponding values of
\vec{q}^{u_{\delta}}
be strong enough to force that compatibility is witnessed by these projections
\dot{\rho}_{\alpha_{j}\alpha_{i}}
in the desired way. Since Player II only increases the length of the conditions by one, it is easy for them to maintain condition (
4
). But at a limit step of cofinality
\mu
, we cannot find a single condition forcing the desired properties of the names on a cofinal subset.

As
v
and
\vec{u}=\langle u_{\zeta}:\zeta<\delta\rangle
belong to
H_{\mu^{+}}
of the generic extension, there is a forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
appearing in
G_{2}
such that
v
and
\vec{u}
are added by
\mathbb{P}
. Without loss of generality,
\mathbb{P}
is of the form

\mathbb{T}\ast\dot{\mathbb{R}}
where
\langle\mathbb{T}\ast\dot{\mathbb{R}},1,\sigma\rangle=\psi(1,\langle\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{i},1,\dot{\varsigma}_{i}\rangle)
for some
i<\lambda
, and it is the maximal coordinate of
v
.
Let
\alpha_{\delta}+1
be the length of
v
.
Pick
\mathbb{P}
-names
\dot{v}
for
v
and
\dot{u}^{\prime}_{\delta}
for the coordinate-wise union of the elements of
\vec{u}
.
As
\mathbb{P}
collapses
\alpha_{\delta}
and
\xi_{\delta}
to
\mu
, we may fix enumerations
\vec{\alpha}=\langle\hat{\alpha}_{i}:i<\mu\rangle
of
\{\alpha_{j}:j<\delta\}
and
\vec{\xi}=\langle\hat{\xi}_{i}:i<\mu\rangle
of
\xi_{\delta}
in the generic extension by
\mathbb{P}
. Let
f:\mu\to\mu
be such that
\xi_{f(i)}+1
is the length of Player II’s move with top poset
\mathbb{T}*\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{\hat{\alpha}_{i}}
.

Let
\langle\langle t_{i},\dot{r}_{i}\rangle:i<\mu\rangle
be a descending sequence contained in the generic of
\mathbb{T}*\dot{\mathbb{R}}
such that for every
i<\mu
,
\langle t_{i},\dot{r}_{i}\rangle
decides the values of

f\restriction i,\vec{\alpha}\restriction i,\vec{\xi}\restriction i,\dot{v}\restriction\{\hat{\alpha}_{j}:j<i\}\cup\{\alpha_{\delta}\},
and
\dot{u}^{\prime}_{\delta}\restriction\{\hat{\xi}_{j}:j<i\}
.
Moreover, let us assume that
\langle t_{i},\dot{r}_{i}\rangle
forces that for all
j<i
,

\langle\mathbb{P}_{\hat{\xi}_{f(j)}},1,\tau_{\hat{\xi}_{f(j)}}\rangle=\psi(1,\langle\dot{\mathbb{R}}_{\hat{\alpha}_{j}},1,\dot{\varsigma}_{\hat{\alpha}_{j}}\rangle)
, and
if
j,k<i
and
\hat{\xi}_{f(k)}<\hat{\xi}_{f(j)}
, then
\pi^{u^{\prime}_{\delta}}_{\hat{\xi}_{f(j)},\hat{\xi}_{f(k)}}=\mathrm{id}\times\dot{\rho}_{\hat{\alpha}_{j},\hat{\alpha}_{k}}
.

Let
u_{\delta}
be an extension of
u^{\prime}_{\delta}
with
\mathbb{P}
at its top coordinate and the following projections. For
\zeta<\xi_{\delta}
, let
\hat{\zeta}
be the ordinal
i
that minimizes
\hat{\xi}_{f(i)}\geq\zeta
, and let
\pi^{u_{\delta}}_{\xi_{\delta},\zeta}=\pi^{u^{\prime}_{\delta}}_{\hat{\xi}_{f(\hat{\zeta})},\zeta}\circ(\mathrm{id}\times\dot{\rho}_{\alpha_{\delta},\alpha_{\hat{\zeta}}})
, restricted to the set of points at which
all compositions of maps from

\{\mathrm{id}\times\dot{\rho}_{\alpha_{\delta},\hat{\alpha}_{i}}:i\leq\hat{\zeta}\}\cup\{\pi^{u^{\prime}_{\delta}}_{jk}:k<j\wedge j,k\in\{\hat{\xi}_{f(i)}:i\leq\hat{\zeta}\}\cup\{\zeta\}\}

are defined.

Let
q^{u_{\delta}}_{\delta,\zeta}=\langle t,\dot{r}\rangle
be a condition in the generic of
\mathbb{T}*\dot{\mathbb{R}}
such that, for the least ordinal
i
that is closed under
f
and such that
\zeta,\hat{\xi}_{f(\hat{\zeta})}
appear in
\vec{\xi}
before
i
and
\alpha_{\hat{\zeta}}
appears in
\vec{\alpha}
before
i
,

•

\langle t,\dot{r}\rangle\leq\langle t_{i},\dot{r}_{i}\rangle
.

•

t
forces
\dot{r}
to be stronger than

\dot{r}_{\alpha_{\delta},\hat{\alpha}_{j}}
for every
j<i
, and to project below
\dot{r}_{\hat{\alpha}_{j},\hat{\alpha}_{k}}
for every
j,k<i
such that
\hat{\alpha}_{k}<\hat{\alpha}_{j}
.

•

\langle t,\dot{r}\rangle
projects below
q^{u^{\prime}_{\delta}}_{jk}
whenever
k<j
and
j,k\in\{\hat{\xi}_{f(l)}:l<i\}\cup\{\zeta\}
.

Let
x\in[\xi_{\delta}]^{<\mu}
. We would like to verify requirement (
4
) with respect to
x
. Note that as no new sequences of ordinals of length
<\mu
are introduced by
G_{2}
,
x\in V
.

Let us first assume that
x\subseteq\{\xi_{\gamma}:\gamma\text{ is odd and finite or infinite and even}\}
. Let
q
be a condition projecting below
q^{u_{\delta}}_{\xi_{\gamma_{0}},\xi_{\gamma_{1}}}
for all
\xi_{\gamma_{0}},\xi_{\gamma_{1}}\in x\cup\{\xi_{\delta}\}
. In particular, for every
\xi_{\gamma_{0}},\xi_{\gamma_{1}}\in x
,
q
is stronger than a condition
\langle t_{i},r_{i}\rangle
that forces
that
\pi^{u_{\delta}}_{\xi_{\gamma_{0}},\xi_{\gamma_{1}}}=\mathrm{id}\times\dot{\rho}_{\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k}}
for some
k<j<\delta
, and the first coordinate (
\mathbb{T}
-part) of
q
forces that the second coordinate projects below
\dot{r}_{\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k}}
.

Let
\vec{s}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}\restriction x,\vec{\pi}\restriction x)
be stronger than the projection of
q
. Then, for every
\xi_{\gamma}\in x
,
\vec{s}(\xi_{\gamma})\in\mathbb{T}\ast\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{\gamma}}
. As the projections to the first coordinate are the identity, those projections are the same for all
\xi_{\gamma}\in x
and stronger than the projection of
q
to
\mathbb{T}
. The projections of the second coordinate are done according to the projections
\rho_{\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k}}
.

As
q
forces
v
to be a condition and forces the projections in
u_{\delta}
to be of the form
\mathrm{id}\times\dot{\rho}_{\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k}}
, we conclude that we can strengthen the second coordinate of
q
to obtain some
q^{\prime}
that projects below
\vec{s}
.

Let us now deal with the general case. Similarly to the proof of Lemma
40
, we first extend
x
to
x\cup y
, where
y
is contained in the indices of posets played by Player II
and
|y|<\mu
, and then apply the argument for
y
, using commutativity in order to obtain the a condition whose restriction to
x
is as desired.

In this case, let
y=\{\hat{\xi}_{f(\hat{\zeta})}:\zeta\in x\}
. The argument that we can extend
\vec{s}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}\restriction x,\vec{\pi}\restriction x)
to
\vec{t}\in\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{P}}\restriction(x\cup y),\vec{\pi}\restriction(x\cup y))
is identical to the argument of Lemma
40
. The main difference is that in this case,
y
is not the list of
all
steps of Player II but rather just those with index
\hat{\xi}_{f(\hat{\zeta})}
for
\zeta\in x
.
So, we must verify that the
\mathbb{T}
-part of
q
forces that the relevant conditions
\dot{r}_{\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k}}
are weaker than the projections of the second coordinate of
q
to posets indexed by
y
. This is ensured by that way that
q
was constructed: For each such pair, let
i
be the index in which the last one of them is enumerated. Then
q=\langle t,\dot{r}\rangle
is stronger than
\langle t_{i},\dot{r}_{i}\rangle
, and
t
forces that
\dot{r}
projects below
\dot{r}_{\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k}}
for the relevant ordinals
j,k
.
∎

Theorem 54
.

Suppose
\kappa
is almost-huge with target
\lambda
and
\mu<\kappa
is regular uncountable. Then
\text{\char 87\relax}(\mu,\kappa)*\dot{\mathbb{U}}(\dot{G}_{0})*\dot{\text{\char 87\relax}}(\kappa,\lambda)*\dot{\mathbb{U}}(\dot{G}_{1})
forces that there is a normal ideal
I
on
\kappa
such that
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\kappa))
.

Proof.

Let
j:V\to M
be an embedding witnessing that
\kappa
is almost-huge with target
\lambda
. Let us assume that
j(\lambda)<\lambda^{+}
and
j[\lambda]
is cofinal in
j(\lambda)
.

Suppose
G_{0}*U_{0}*G_{1}*U_{1}\subseteq\text{\char 87\relax}(\mu,\kappa)*\dot{\mathbb{U}}(\dot{G}_{0})*\dot{\text{\char 87\relax}}(\kappa,\lambda)*\dot{\mathbb{U}}(\dot{G}_{1})
is generic over
V
, and
K\subseteq\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\kappa)
is generic over
V[G_{0}{*}U_{0}{*}G_{1}{*}U_{1}]
. Let
\mathbb{T}
be the tree forcing of Lemma
43
, and let
B\subseteq\mathbb{T}
be the generic corresponding to
G_{0}*U_{0}*K
. Let
\psi(B{*}G_{1})=G_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq\text{\char 87\relax}(\mu,\lambda)
, where
\psi
is the projection of Lemma
49
. By Lemma
43
,
G_{0}\subseteq G_{0}^{\prime}
. Thus the embedding can be lifted to
j:V[G_{0}]\to M[G_{0}^{\prime}]
. Since every set of ordinals of size
{<}\lambda
in
V[B{*}G_{1}{*}U_{1}]
is in
V[(G_{0}^{\prime})^{\tau}_{\mathbb{P}}]
for some coding condition
\langle\mathbb{P},1,\tau\rangle\in G_{0}^{\prime}
,
M[G_{0}^{\prime}]
is
{<}\lambda
-closed in
V[B{*}G_{1}{*}U_{1}]
.

Let
u^{*}
be the condition in
\mathbb{U}(G_{0}^{\prime})
given by Lemma
43
. Since
|\mathcal{P}(\lambda)^{M[G_{0}^{\prime}]}|=\lambda
in
V[B{*}G_{1}]
, and
\mathbb{U}(G_{0}^{\prime})
is
\lambda
-strategically closed in
V[B{*}G_{1}{*}U_{1}]
by Lemma
53
, we can build a filter
U_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{U}(G_{0}^{\prime})
with
u^{*}\in U_{0}^{\prime}
that is generic over
M[G_{0}^{\prime}]
. By Lemma
43
, we have
U_{0}\subseteq U_{0}^{\prime}
, and thus the embedding can be lifted further to
j:V[G_{0}{*}U_{0}]\to M[G_{0}^{\prime}{*}U_{0}^{\prime}]
.

Next, we use
U_{1}
just as in the proof of Theorem
47
to build a descending sequence
\langle q_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\rangle\subseteq\text{\char 87\relax}(\lambda,j(\lambda))^{M[G_{0}^{\prime}*U_{0}^{\prime}]}
such that:

•

for every
p\in G_{1}
, there is
\alpha<\lambda
such that
q_{\alpha}\leq j(p)
, and

•

for every
X\subseteq\kappa
in
V[G_{0}{*}U_{0}{*}G_{1}]
and every name
\tau\in V[G_{0}{*}U_{0}]
such that
\tau^{G}=X
, there is
\alpha<\lambda
such that
q_{\alpha}
decides whether
\kappa\in j(\tau)
.

The construction is the same, so we will not repeat it, but we note that to carry out the argument we need that all
{<}\lambda
-sized subsets of
G_{1}
are in
M[G_{0}^{\prime}]
, which is guaranteed by Lemma
52
and the closure of
M[G_{0}^{\prime}]
. Then we define an ultrafilter
F
on
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)^{V[G_{0}{*}U_{0}{*}G_{1}]}
by
X\in F
iff for some name
\tau
such that
\tau^{G}=X
and some
\alpha<\lambda
,
q_{\alpha}\Vdash\check{\kappa}\in j(\tau)
. As before,
F
is a
V[G_{0}{*}U_{0}{*}G_{1}]
-normal ultrafilter.
In
V[G_{0}{*}U_{0}{*}G_{1}{*}U_{1}]
, we define an ideal
I
on
\kappa
by
X\in I
iff
\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\kappa)
forces
X\notin\dot{F}
.
I
is a normal ideal, and
e:[X]_{I}\mapsto||X\in\dot{F}||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\kappa))}
is a complete embedding. It follows that
I
is
\kappa
-dense.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\kappa))
. For this, it suffices to show that there is a name
\tau
forcing language of
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I
such that, whenever
K\subseteq\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\kappa)
is generic, if
F=e^{-1}[K]
, then
K=\tau^{F}
. This is very similar to Claim
45
. Since unlike in Claim
45
, we did not specify an
M
-generic for
j(\text{\char 87\relax}(\mu,\kappa)*\mathbb{U}(\dot{G}_{0})*\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)*\mathbb{U}(\dot{G}_{1}))
, we need to be a bit more careful about our elementary embeddings.

Let
K,B,G_{0}^{\prime},U_{0}^{\prime}
be as above, and let
F=e^{-1}[K]
. Let
F_{0}=F\cap V[G_{0}{*}U_{0}]
. There is
\alpha_{0}<\lambda
such that
q_{\alpha_{0}}
decides
F_{0}
as the collection of
X\in\mathcal{P}(\kappa)^{V[G_{0}*U_{0}]}
for which
\kappa\in j(X)
. Let
i_{0}:V[G_{0}{*}U_{0}]\to N_{0}=\Ult(V[G_{0}{*}U_{0}],F_{0})
be the ultrapower embedding. There is an elementary map
k_{0}:N_{0}\to M[G_{0}^{\prime}{*}U_{0}^{\prime}]
given by
k_{0}([h]_{F_{0}})=j(h)(\kappa)
, and
j\restriction V[G_{0}{*}U_{0}]=k_{0}\circ i_{0}
. Since
\crit(i_{0})=\kappa
,
\kappa=\mu^{+}
in
V[G_{0}]
, and the critical point of
k_{0}
is a cardinal of
V[G_{0}]
above
\mu
,
\crit(k_{0})
is greater than
\kappa+\kappa
. Recall that
G_{0}^{\prime}
possesses a strong coding condition of the form
\langle\mathbb{T},1,\tau^{*}\rangle
, where
\tau^{*}
is forced to have length
\kappa+\kappa
, and
(G_{0}^{\prime})^{\tau^{*}}_{\mathbb{T}}=B
.
Since
\mathbb{T},\tau^{*}\in V
and these are coded by subsets of
\kappa
, they are in
N_{0}
.
By the elementarity of
k_{0}
,
\langle\mathbb{T},1,\tau^{*}\rangle\in i_{0}(G_{0})
, and moreover
i_{0}(G_{0})^{\tau^{*}}_{\mathbb{T}}=B
.

K
can be read off as the filter on the third coordinate of the decomposition of
\mathbb{T}
given by Lemma
43
. Therefore,
K\in N_{0}
and there is a
(\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I)
-name
\tau
that evaluates to
K
, as desired.
∎

Corollary 55
.

Suppose
\langle\kappa_{n}:n<\omega\rangle
is a sequence such that for all
n
,
\kappa_{n}
is almost-huge with target
\kappa_{n+1}
. Then
some forcing extension satisfies that for every
n<\omega
, there is a normal ideal
I
on
\omega_{n+1}
such that

\mathcal{P}(\omega_{n+1})/I\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\omega_{n},\omega_{n+1})).

Suppose
\kappa
is a huge cardinal, and
j:V\to M
is an elementary embedding such that
\crit(j)=\kappa
and
M^{j(\kappa)}\subseteq M
.
If
\mathcal{U}
is the normal ultrafilter on
\kappa
derived from
j
, then a standard reflection argument (using the characterization of almost-hugeness from
[
19
, Theorem 24.11]
) shows that there is a set
A\in\mathcal{U}
such that for
\alpha<\beta
from
A
,
\alpha
is almost-huge with target
\beta
.
A similar argument shows that for every
A\subseteq\kappa
, there is
X\in\mathcal{U}
such that for all
\alpha\in X
, there is an embedding
i:V\to N
witnessing that
\alpha
is almost-huge with target
\kappa
, and
i(A\cap\alpha)=A
.

Theorem 56
.

If
\theta
is a huge cardinal, then there is a generic extension in which
\theta
remains inaccessible, there is a stationary set of measurable cardinals below
\theta
,
and
V_{\theta}
satisfies that for all regular cardinals
\mu
, there is a normal ideal
I
on
\mu^{+}
such that

\mathcal{P}(\mu^{+})/I\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\mu^{+})).
12
12

12

Shioya told the first author that the question of whether there can exist a dense ideal at the successor of a measurable cardinal was raised by Woodin at the 2004 workshop “Singular Cardinal Combinatorics” at the Banff International Research Station.

Proof.

Let
j:V\to M
witness that
\theta
is huge with target
\lambda
. Let
A\subseteq\theta
be such that for all
\alpha<\beta
in
A
,
\alpha
is almost-huge with target
\beta
.

Define an iteration
\langle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha},\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{\alpha}:\alpha<\theta\rangle
as follows. Enumerate the elements of the closure of
A
as
\langle\kappa_{\alpha}:\alpha<\theta\rangle
. Let
\mathbb{Q}_{0}=\mathbb{A}(\omega,\kappa_{0})
, and for
1\leq n<\omega
, let
\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{n}=\dot{\text{\char 87\relax}}(\kappa_{n-1},\kappa_{n})*\dot{\mathbb{U}}(\dot{G}_{n})
, where
\dot{G}_{n}
is a name for the generic on the first coordinate of this pair. If
\alpha
is a limit and
\kappa_{\alpha}
is singular, let
\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{\alpha}=\dot{\text{\char 87\relax}}(\kappa_{\alpha}^{+},\kappa_{\alpha+1})*\dot{\mathbb{U}}(\dot{G}_{\alpha})
, where
\dot{G}_{\alpha}
is a name for the generic on the first coordinate. If
\alpha
is a limit and
\kappa_{\alpha}
is inaccessible, let
\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{\alpha}=\dot{\text{\char 87\relax}}(\kappa_{\alpha},\kappa_{\alpha+1})*\dot{\mathbb{U}}(\dot{G}_{\alpha})
, where
\dot{G}_{\alpha}
is a name for the generic on the first coordinate. Take the limits
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
with Easton support.
Using the strategic closure of
\text{\char 87\relax}(\alpha,\beta)*\dot{\mathbb{U}}(\dot{G})
given by Lemma
40
, standard arguments show that
\theta
remains inaccessible and the set of regular uncountable cardinals below
\theta
becomes the set of ordinals
\kappa_{\alpha}
for successor
\alpha
,
\kappa_{\alpha}^{+}
when
\kappa_{\alpha}
is a singular limit, and
\kappa_{\alpha}
when
\kappa_{\alpha}
is inaccessible.
By Theorem
54
,
\mathbb{P}_{\theta}
forces that for all regular cardinals
\mu<\theta
there is a normal ideal
I
on
\mu^{+}
such that
\mathcal{P}(\mu^{+})/I\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\mu^{+}))
.

To show that stationary-many measurable cardinals below
\theta
are preserved, let
\delta<\theta
be such that there is an almost-huge embedding
i:V\to M
with
\crit(i)=\delta
,
i(\delta)=\theta
, and
i(A\cap\delta)=A
. Since the set of such cardinals is stationary in
V
and
\mathbb{P}_{\theta}
is
\theta
-c.c., it suffices to show the measurability of such cardinals is preserved. Let
H\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\theta}
be generic, and let
H_{\alpha}=H\restriction\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha<\theta
. The embedding
i
easily lifts to
i:V[H_{\delta}]\to N[H]
.

Next, we must lift the embedding through
H_{\delta+1}
. Let
H(\delta)=G_{\delta}*U_{\delta}\subseteq\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa_{\delta},\kappa_{\delta+1})*\dot{\mathbb{U}}(\dot{G}_{\delta})
. Let
\langle\langle\mathbb{R}_{\alpha},1,\tau_{\alpha}\rangle:\alpha<\kappa_{\delta+1}\rangle
,
\langle\pi_{\beta\alpha}:\alpha<\beta<\kappa_{\delta+1}\rangle
, and
\langle q_{\beta\alpha}:\alpha<\beta<\kappa_{\delta+1}\rangle
be the sequences given by
U_{\delta}
.
For
\alpha<\kappa_{\delta+1}
, let
m_{\alpha}=\inf\{i(p):p\in(G_{\delta})^{\tau_{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{R}_{\alpha}}\}
, and let
\vec{m}=\langle m_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa_{\delta+1}\rangle
. Note that for all
\alpha<\beta<\kappa_{\delta+1}
,
m_{\alpha}=i(\pi_{\beta\alpha})(m_{\beta})
, and
m_{\beta}\leq i(q_{\beta\alpha})
. In
N[H]
, the pair

\langle\vec{\mathbb{R}},\vec{\pi}\rangle=\langle\langle i(\mathbb{R}_{\alpha})\restriction m_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa_{\delta+1}\rangle,\langle i(\pi_{\beta\alpha}):\alpha<\beta<\kappa_{\delta+1}\rangle\rangle

is a
\theta
-good inverse system of length
\kappa_{\delta+1}<\theta
.

For each
\alpha<\kappa_{\delta+1}
, it is possible to lift the embedding to
i:V[H_{\delta}][(G_{\delta})^{\tau_{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{R}_{\alpha}}]\to N[H][K]
by further forcing, and using the fact that
U_{\delta}\in N[H]
,
the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
47
shows that
\langle\vec{\mathbb{R}},\vec{\pi}\rangle
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
16
. Thus for
\alpha<\kappa_{\theta+1}
, the map
\vec{p}\mapsto\vec{p}(\alpha)
is a projection from
\langle\vec{\mathbb{R}},\vec{\pi}\rangle
to
i(\mathbb{R}_{\alpha})\restriction m_{\alpha}
, and it makes sense to define a
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{R}},\vec{\pi})
-name
\tau^{*}
as the concatenation of all
i(\tau_{\alpha})
for
\alpha<\kappa_{\theta+1}
, as evaluated through these projections.

Let
\gamma=\sup i[\kappa_{\delta+1}]
, and let
\mathbb{Q}
be the disjoint sum of
\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{R}},\vec{\pi})
and
\mathrm{Col}(\theta,{<}\gamma)
, so that for a maximal antichain
\{q_{0},q_{1}\}
,
\mathbb{Q}\restriction q_{0}=\varprojlim(\vec{\mathbb{R}},\vec{\pi})
and
\mathbb{Q}\restriction q_{1}=\mathrm{Col}(\theta,{<}\gamma)
. Define a projection
\sigma_{\alpha}:\mathbb{Q}\to i(\mathbb{R}_{\alpha})
by putting
\sigma_{\alpha}
below
q_{0}
as the canonical restriction map, and
\sigma_{\alpha}
below
q_{1}
as anything that projects to
i(\mathbb{R}_{\alpha})
, which can be done since
\gamma>|i(\mathbb{R}_{\alpha})|
. Amend
\tau^{*}
to ensure that
q_{1}
also forces it to code the generic.
Hence
\langle\mathbb{Q},q_{0},\tau^{*}\rangle
is a coding condition in
\text{\char 87\relax}(\theta,i(\kappa_{\delta+1}))^{N[H]}
below
i[G_{\delta}]
. Thus we may lift the embedding further to
i:V[H_{\delta}][G_{\delta}]\to N[H][K]
whenever
K
is a
\text{\char 87\relax}(\theta,i(\kappa_{\delta+1}))^{N[H]}
-generic possessing
\langle\mathbb{Q},q_{0},\tau^{*}\rangle
. Then we may argue similarly as in the limit case of Lemma
40
that a lower bound
u^{*}
to
i[U_{\delta}]
can be constructed with
\langle\mathbb{Q},1,\tau^{*}\rangle
at the top. Thus there exists a lower bound
p^{*}
to
i[G_{\delta}{*}U_{\delta}]
.

Thus by forcing with

i(\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa_{\delta},\kappa_{\delta+1})1*\dot{\mathbb{U}}(\dot{G}_{\delta}))^{N[H]}
below
p^{*}
, we can lift the embedding
i
. In this model, let
\mathcal{U}=\{X\subseteq\delta:\delta\in i(X)\}
. Then
\mathcal{U}
is a
V[H_{\delta+1}]
-normal ultrafilter. Since the last forcing was
\delta^{++}
-distributive,
\mathcal{U}\in V[H]
. Since
\mathcal{P}(\delta)^{V[H]}=\mathcal{P}(\delta)^{V[H_{\delta+1}]}
,
\mathcal{U}
is a normal measure over
\delta
in
V[H]
.
∎

Working a bit harder, it is possible to show that even stronger large cardinal properties are preserved. But we will leave the details of this exploration to a future work.

7
Applications

7.1
GCH, diamond, and reflection

Proposition 57
.

Suppose
\mu,\alpha<\kappa
,
I
is a
\kappa
-complete ideal on
X
, and the forcing
\mathcal{P}(X)/I
adds no elements of
[\alpha]^{<\mu}
. Then
\alpha^{<\mu}<\kappa
.

Proof.

Let
G\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)/I
be generic, and let
j_{G}:V\to\Ult(V,G)
be the generic ultrapower embedding. We do not assume that the ultrapower is well-founded, but it is well-founded up to
\kappa
, and
\kappa\leq\crit(j_{G})
(see
[
14
]
). Let
\vec{x}=\langle x_{\beta}:\beta<\delta\rangle
be an enumeration of
[\alpha]^{<\mu}
. If
\delta\geq\kappa
then
j_{G}(\vec{x})([\mathrm{id}]_{G})
is a member of
[\alpha]^{<\mu}
in
V[G]
, and
j_{G}(\vec{x})([\mathrm{id}]_{G})\not=x_{\beta}=j(\vec{x})(j(\beta))
for any
\beta<\delta
. This contradicts the assumption that
\mathcal{P}(X)/I
adds no elements of
[\alpha]^{<\mu}
.
∎

It follows that if
\mu
is regular,
\kappa=\mu^{+}
and
I
is a
\kappa
-complete ideal on
\kappa
such that
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I
is forcing-equivalent to
\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\kappa)
, then
\kappa^{<\mu}=\kappa
, and therefore
I
is
\kappa
-dense and thus
\kappa^{+}
-saturated. By Solovay
[
28
]
, whenever
G\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I
is generic,
\Ult(V,G)
is isomorphic to a transitive class
M\subseteq V[G]
that is closed under
\mu
-sequences from
V[G]
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I
is a complete Boolean algebra by Tarski
[
29
]
.

Proposition 58
.

If
\mu>\omega
is regular,
\kappa=\mu^{+}
and
I
is a
\kappa
-complete ideal on
\kappa
such that
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I
is forcing-equivalent to
\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\kappa)
, then
\diamondsuit_{\mu}(S)
holds for all stationary
S\subseteq\mu
.

Proof.

Let
g:\mu\to\kappa
be a generic surjection corresponding to a
\mathrm{Col}(\mu,\kappa)
-generic filter. In
V[g]
, define a sequence
\vec{a}=\langle a_{\alpha}:\alpha<\mu\rangle
by
a_{\alpha}=\{\beta<\alpha:g(\alpha+\beta)=0\}
. It is a standard exercise to show that, for all stationary
S\subseteq\mu
in
V
,
\langle a_{\alpha}:\alpha\in S\rangle
is a
\diamondsuit_{\mu}(S)
-sequence in
V[g]
.

Let
j:V\to M
be the ultrapower embedding by the associated generic filter on
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I
. By the closure of
M
,
\vec{a}\in M
, and for each stationary
S\subseteq\mu
from
V
,
M
satisfies that
\vec{a}\restriction S
is a
\diamondsuit_{\mu}(S)
-sequence. Thus
M\models\diamondsuit_{\mu}(S)
for each such
S
, and by elementarity and the fact that
\crit(j)>\mu
,
V\models\diamondsuit_{\mu}(S)
for all stationary
S\subseteq\mu
.
∎

Proposition 59
.

Suppose
\omega<\mu<\kappa
are regular, and
I
is a
\kappa
-complete ideal on
\kappa
such that
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I
is equivalent to a
\mu
-closed forcing. Then for every sequence of stationary sets
\langle S_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
such that each
S_{\alpha}\subseteq\kappa\cap\cof({<}\mu)
, there is
\delta<\kappa
such that for all
\alpha<\delta
,
S_{\alpha}\cap\delta
is stationary in
\delta
.

Proof.

By Proposition
57
,
\alpha^{<\mu}<\kappa
for all
\alpha<\kappa
, and thus by a well-known argument using sufficiently closed elementary submodels,
\mu
-closed forcing preserves every stationary
S\subseteq\kappa\cap\cof({<}\mu)
(see for example,
[
24
]
).
It follows from
[
14
, Proposition 2.7]
that if
\mathcal{P}(X)/I
is equivalent to a countably closed forcing, then it is precipitous (i.e. generic ultrapowers are always well-founded).

Let
G\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I
be generic, and let
j:V\to M
be the ultrapower embedding, where
M
is transitive. Let
\vec{S}=\langle S_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
be as hypothesized, and let
\vec{T}=j(\vec{S})
. Then for every
\alpha<\kappa
,
j(S_{\alpha})\cap\kappa=S_{\alpha}
is stationary in
V[G]
, and so
M\models(\forall\alpha<\kappa)\vec{T}(\alpha)\cap\kappa
is stationary in
\kappa
. The conclusion follows by elementarity.
∎

The same argument shows that the reflection principle
\mathrm{DSR}({<}\mu,\kappa\cap\cof({<}\mu))
, which was introduced by Fuchs
[
16
]
, holds. By
[
16
, Theorem 3.4]
, the conclusion of the above proposition implies the failure of the principle
\square(\kappa,{<}\mu)
.
13
13

13

In Fuchs’ definition, we require the existence of an ordinal
\gamma
, such that
S_{\alpha}\cap\gamma
is stationary for club many
\alpha<\gamma
. In our case, we can take this club to be
\gamma
itself and thus we obtain
\mathrm{DSR}({<}\kappa,\kappa\cap\cof({<}\mu))
.

Let
\lambda
be a cardinal. Recall that a partial
C
-sequence on
S\subseteq\lambda
is a sequence of the form
\langle C_{\alpha}:\alpha\in S\rangle
where
C_{\alpha}
is closed and cofinal at
\alpha
. A partial
C
-sequence is
coherent
if for every
\alpha\in S
, and
\beta\in\acc C_{\alpha}
, we have that
\beta\in S
and
C_{\beta}=C_{\alpha}\cap\beta
.

A coherent partial
C
-sequence with domain
S
is
trivial
if there is a club
D
such that
\forall\gamma\in\acc D\cap S
,
D\cap\gamma=C_{\gamma}
.
14
14

14

In this case, if
S
is stationary then if follows that
\acc D\subseteq S
, using coherency.

Let
S^{\lambda}_{\leq\kappa}=\{\alpha<\lambda\mid\cf\alpha\leq\kappa\}
.

Lemma 60
.

Let
\kappa
be an uncountable regular cardinal and let
\lambda>\kappa^{+}
be regular. Let us assume that there is a saturated ideal
I
on
\kappa^{+}
such that
\mathcal{P}(\kappa^{+})/I
contains a
\sigma
-closed dense set.

Then, every coherent partial
C
-sequence
\langle C_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in S\rangle
,
S^{\lambda}_{\leq\kappa}\subseteq S
, can be (uniquely) extended to a coherent partial
C
-sequence with domain containing
S^{\lambda}_{\leq\kappa^{+}}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\langle C_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in S\rangle
,
S\supseteq S^{\lambda}_{\leq\kappa}
, be a coherent partial
C
-sequence.

Let
H\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\kappa^{+})/I
be
V
-generic. In
V[H]
, there is an elementary embedding
j\colon V\to M
, with
\crit j=\kappa^{+}
and
M^{\kappa}\cap V[H]\subseteq M
(by the saturation of
I
).

Fix
\delta<\lambda
,
\cf\delta=\kappa^{+}
. Let us look at
j(\mathcal{C})
and let
\tilde{\delta}=\sup j[\delta]
. By the closure of
M
,
\cf^{M}\tilde{\delta}=\kappa=j(\kappa)
and thus
D=j(\mathcal{C})_{\tilde{\delta}}
is a club. Let
C_{\delta}
be the closure of
j^{-1}[D]:=\{\alpha:j(\alpha)\in D\}
. We need to show that
\sup C_{\delta}=\delta
and that the extended partial
C
-sequence is coherent, namely for every
\gamma\in\acc C_{\delta}
,
C_{\delta}\cap\gamma=C_{\gamma}
.

First, since the embedding
j
is
\sigma
-continuous,
j[\delta]
and
D
are both
\sigma
-clubs at
\tilde{\delta}
and thus must intersect cofinally.

Next, for
\gamma\in\acc C_{\delta}
such that
\cf^{V}\gamma\neq\kappa^{+}
,
\sup j[\gamma]=j(\gamma)
and thus
j(\gamma)
is an accumulation point of
D
.
15
15

15

If
\cf\gamma\leq\kappa=\crit j
, continuity is clear. For
\cf\gamma\geq\kappa^{++}
, this follows from the assumption that the ideal is on
\kappa^{+}
.
By the coherency of
j(\mathcal{C})
,
D\cap j(\gamma)=j(\mathcal{C})_{j(\gamma)}=j(C_{\gamma})
. Therefore,
j^{-1}[D]\cap\gamma=C_{\gamma}
which is already closed.
16
16

16

Note that there is always an accumulation point
\tilde{\gamma}
of
C_{\delta}
of countable cofinality above
\gamma
, so
\tilde{\gamma}\in S
. In particular,
\gamma\in\acc C_{\tilde{\gamma}}
and thus in
S
.

To show coherence for
\gamma
of cofinality
\kappa^{+}
, let us first show that
C_{\delta}
is unique in the following strong sense. Let
E
be a club at
\delta
in any extension
W
of
V[H]
by a
\sigma
-closed forcing, such that for every
\gamma\in\acc E\cap(S^{\lambda}_{\leq\kappa})^{V}
,
E\cap\gamma=C_{\gamma}
. Then
E=C_{\delta}
.

Indeed, in
W
,
\cf\delta>\omega
and
S^{\delta}_{\omega}
is still stationary at
\delta
.
So,
\acc E\cap\acc C_{\delta}\cap S^{\delta}_{\omega}
is cofinal at
\delta
. For every
\gamma\in\acc E\cap\acc C_{\delta}\cap S^{\delta}_{\omega}
,
E\cap\gamma=C_{\delta}\cap\gamma
.

Now, applying the uniqueness for
W=V[H]
, and assuming that
\gamma
is of
V
-cofinality
\kappa^{+}
and
\gamma\in\acc C_{\delta}
. Then as
C_{\delta}\cap\gamma
satisfies the requirements with respect to
\gamma
, it must be equal to
C_{\gamma}
.

Finally, let
H^{\prime}
be
V[H]
-generic filter for
\mathcal{P}(\kappa^{+})/I
. So, working in
V[H^{\prime}]
, we obtain a club
C^{\prime}_{\delta}
threading
\mathcal{C}\restriction\delta
. Applying uniqueness for
W=V[H][H^{\prime}]
,
C_{\delta}^{\prime}=C_{\delta}
. Therefore,
C_{\delta}\in V[H]\cap V[H^{\prime}]=V
. By uniqueness, the map
\delta\mapsto C_{\delta}
for
\delta\in S^{\lambda}_{\kappa^{+}}
belongs to
V
.
∎

Theorem 61
.

Assume that for every
n<\omega
there is an ideal
I_{n}
such that
\mathcal{P}(\omega_{n+1})/I_{n}\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\omega_{n},\omega_{n+1}))
. Then, for every
m\geq 2
, every partial coherent
C
-sequence
\mathcal{C}=\langle C_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in S\rangle
such that
S\supseteq S^{\omega_{m}}_{\leq\omega_{1}}
is trivial.

Proof.

By Lemma
60
, any such partial
C
-sequence can be extended uniquely to a coherent
C
-sequence with full domain. If the obtained sequence is non-trivial, then it is a
\square(\omega_{m})
-sequence, and in particular it is incompatible with the existence of a
\sigma
-closed ideal on
\omega_{m}
by Proposition
59
.
∎

Let us remark that given a model with a dense ideal on
\kappa^{+}
, one can add (using forcing) a square sequence
\square_{\kappa^{+}}
by initial segments. This forcing does not add any subset to
\kappa^{+}
, so the ideal remains dense in the generic extension.

7.2
Boolean transfer

We say that an ideal on
X
is
uniform
if every
Y\subseteq X
of size
<|X|
is in the ideal. A filter is said to be uniform when its dual ideal is uniform.

Proposition 62
.

If
\kappa\leq\lambda
are infinite cardinals and
I
is an ideal on
\kappa
, then there exists a uniform ideal
J
on
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I\cong\mathcal{P}(\lambda)/J
. If
I
is
\delta
-complete and there exists a
\delta
-complete uniform ultrafilter on
\lambda
, then
J
can be taken to be
\delta
-complete.

Proof.

Let
\langle X_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle
be a partition of
\lambda
into
\kappa
-many pieces of size
\lambda
. For each
\alpha<\kappa
, let
U_{\alpha}
be a uniform ultrafilter on
X_{\alpha}
. Let
J_{0}
be the collection of subsets of
\lambda
defined by
A\in J_{0}
if and only if for all
\alpha<\kappa
,
A\cap X_{\alpha}\notin U_{\alpha}
. Since for each
\alpha<\kappa
,
\mathcal{P}(X_{\alpha})\setminus U_{\alpha}
is an ideal on
X_{\alpha}
,
J_{0}
is an ideal.

Let
\pi:\lambda\to\kappa
be defined by
\pi(\beta)=\alpha
, where
\alpha
is the unique ordinal such that
\beta\in X_{\alpha}
. Let
\varphi:\mathcal{P}(\kappa)\to\mathcal{P}(\lambda)/J_{0}
be defined by
\varphi(B)=[\pi^{-1}[B]]_{J_{0}}
.
It is clear that
\varphi
is an injective Boolean homomorphism. To check that it is surjective, let
A\subseteq\lambda
, and let
B=\{\alpha<\kappa:A\cap X_{\alpha}\in U_{\alpha}\}
. It suffices to show that
A
is mod-
J_{0}
-equivalent to
C=\bigcup_{\alpha\in B}X_{\alpha}
. But
A\setminus C=\bigcup_{\alpha\notin B}A\cap X_{\alpha}\in J_{0}
, and
C\setminus A=\bigcup_{\alpha\in B}X_{\alpha}\setminus A_{\alpha}\in J_{0}
.

Now let
I
be any ideal on
\kappa
. Then
\varphi[I]
is an ideal on the Boolean algebra
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)/J_{0}
, and we define an ideal
J
on
\lambda
by
A\in J
iff
[A]_{J_{0}}\in\varphi[I]
. It is clear that
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I\cong\mathcal{P}(\lambda)/J
.

Finally, suppose that
\delta
is a regular cardinal such that
I
is
\delta
-complete and that each
U_{\alpha}
is
\delta
-complete. Then
J_{0}
is
\delta
-complete. Suppose
\langle A_{\alpha}:\alpha<\eta\rangle\subseteq J
, where
\eta<\delta
. For each
\alpha
, let
B_{\alpha}\subseteq\kappa
be such that
\left[A_{\alpha}\right]_{J_{0}}=\left[\pi^{-1}[B_{\alpha}]\right]_{J_{0}}
. By the
\delta
-completeness of
J_{0}
,

\left[\bigcup_{\alpha<\eta}A_{\alpha}\right]_{J_{0}}=\left[\bigcup_{\alpha<\eta}\pi^{-1}[B_{\alpha}]\right]_{J_{0}}=\left[\pi^{-1}[\bigcup_{\alpha<\eta}B_{\alpha}]\right]_{J_{0}}
.
Since
I
is
\delta
-complete,
\bigcup_{\alpha<\eta}B_{\alpha}\in I
, and thus
\bigcup_{\alpha<\eta}A_{\alpha}\in J
.
∎

The argument for the next result is extracted from
[
12
]
. The claim about transfer via dense ideals is attributed there to Woodin, along with the remark that it has a relatively simple proof, and the bulk of the paper is dedicated to the more complicated proof of Foreman’s general result about very strongly layered ideals. In the argument below, we employ the idea of Theorem 4.1 of
[
12
]
, the “selection hypothesis”, noticing that it allows both a further simplification (by eliminating the “coding condition”) and a strengthened conclusion.

Theorem 63
.

Assume
\kappa
is regular, there is a normal ideal
J
on
\kappa^{+}
such that
\mathcal{P}(\kappa^{+})/J\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\kappa^{+}))
, and
\diamondsuit_{\kappa^{+}}(\cof(\kappa))
holds. Then there is a
\kappa
-complete ideal
K\supseteq J
and a function
\pi:\kappa^{+}\to\kappa
such that:

1.

For all
A\subseteq\kappa
,
|A|<\kappa
iff
\pi^{-1}[A]\in K
.

2.

[A]_{\mathrm{bd}}\mapsto[\pi^{-1}[A]]_{K}
is an isomorphism from
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/\mathrm{bd}
to
\mathcal{P}(\kappa^{+})/K
.

3.

For
\mu\leq\kappa
,
[f]_{\mathrm{bd}}\mapsto[f\circ\pi]_{K}
is a bijection from
\mu^{\kappa}/\mathrm{bd}
to
\mu^{\kappa^{+}}/K
.

Here,
\mathrm{bd}
denotes the ideal of bounded subsets of
\kappa
.

Proof.

Choose a maximal antichain
\langle x_{i}:i<\kappa\rangle
of
\mathbb{B}=\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\kappa^{+}))
.
Fix an isomorphism
\Psi:\mathcal{P}(\kappa^{+})/J\to\mathbb{B}
, and let
\langle X_{i}:i<\kappa\rangle
be a sequence of pairwise disjoint sets such that
\Psi([X_{i}]_{J})=x_{i}
.
Define
\pi:\kappa^{+}\to\kappa
by
\pi(\alpha)=i\iff\alpha\in X_{i}
.
The main ingredient in the proof is the following sequence.

Lemma 64
.

There is a sequence of functions
\vec{f}=\langle f_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa^{+}\rangle
such that:

1.

For each
\alpha
,
f_{\alpha}:\kappa\to\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\kappa^{+})
.

2.

For each
\alpha<\kappa^{+}
and
i<\kappa
,
f_{\alpha}(i)\leq x_{i}
.

3.

For
\alpha<\beta<\kappa^{+}
,
f_{\beta}(i)\leq f_{\alpha}(i)
for all but boundedly-many
i<\kappa
.

4.

For all maximal antichains
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\kappa^{+})
, there is
\alpha<\kappa^{+}
such that for all
i<\kappa
,
f_{\alpha}(i)\leq a
for some
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

Let us assume the sequence
\vec{f}
is given and show how to prove the theorem.
Given such a sequence, we then define an ideal
I
on
\mathbb{B}
by putting
b\in I
when there is
\alpha<\kappa^{+}
such that for all but boundedly many
i<\kappa
,
f_{\alpha}(i)\leq\neg b
.

Let us first check that
I
is a
\kappa
-complete ideal on
\mathbb{B}
. Suppose
\delta<\kappa
and
\{b_{j}:j<\delta\}\subseteq I
. There is
\alpha<\kappa^{+}
and
i_{0}<\kappa
such that whenever
i_{0}\leq i<\kappa
and
j<\delta
,
f_{\alpha}(i)\leq\neg b_{j}
. Thus for
i_{0}\leq i<\kappa
,
f_{\alpha}(i)\leq\prod_{j}\neg b_{j}=\neg\sum_{j}b_{j}
, and so
\sum_{j}b_{j}\in I
.

Let
K=\{A\subseteq\kappa^{+}:\Psi([A]_{J})\in I\}
. We have
\mathcal{P}(\kappa^{+})/K\cong\mathbb{B}/I
. The
\kappa
-completeness of
K
follows from that of both
J
and
I
.
To show that
\pi^{-1}[A]\in K
iff
A
is bounded in
\kappa
, note that for
A\subseteq\kappa
,
\Psi(\pi^{-1}[A]_{J})=\sum_{i\in A}x_{i}
, which is in
I
iff
A
is bounded.

To show that
[A]_{\mathrm{bd}}\mapsto[\pi^{-1}[A]]_{K}
is an isomorphism, it suffices to show that this map is surjective. By (
4
), for any
B\subseteq\kappa^{+}
, there is
\alpha<\kappa^{+}
such that for a cobounded set of
i<\kappa
,
f_{\alpha}(i)\leq\Psi^{-1}([X_{i}\cap B]_{J})
or
f_{\alpha}(i)\leq\Psi^{-1}([X_{i}\setminus B]_{J})
. Let
A=\{i:f_{\alpha}(i)\leq\Psi([B]_{J})\}
.
Thus for a cobounded set of
i
, either
f_{\alpha}(i)
is below both
\Psi([\pi^{-1}[A]]_{J})
and
\Psi([B]_{J})
, or below the meet of their complements, so
[B]_{K}=[\pi^{-1}[A]]_{K}
.

For the claim about reduced powers, first note that if
\mu\leq\kappa
,
f,g\in\mu^{\kappa}
, and
f\not=g
on an unbounded set
A
, then
\pi^{-1}[A]\notin K
, and
f\circ\pi\not=g\circ\pi
on
\pi^{-1}[A]
. To show surjectivity, suppose
g:\kappa^{+}\to\mu
. By the
\kappa^{+}
-completeness of
J
,
\mathcal{A}=\{[g^{-1}\{\gamma\}]_{J}:g^{-1}\{\gamma\}\in J^{+}\}
is a maximal antichain in
\mathcal{P}(\kappa^{+})/J
. By (
4
), there is
\alpha<\kappa^{+}
such that for each
i<\kappa
,
f_{\alpha}(i)\leq\Psi(a)
for some
a\in\mathcal{A}
. For each
i<\kappa
, let
\gamma_{i}
be the unique
\gamma<\mu
such that
f_{\alpha}(i)\leq\Psi([g^{-1}\{\gamma\}]_{J})
Define
h:\kappa\to\mu
by
h(i)=\gamma_{i}
. Then for all
i<\kappa
and all
\beta\in X_{i}
,
h(\pi(\beta))=h(i)=\gamma_{i}
, and
f_{\alpha}(i)\leq\Psi([\{\beta\in X_{i}:g(\beta)=h(\pi(\beta))\}]_{J})
. Thus
[g]_{K}=[h\circ\pi]_{K}
.

It remains to prove Lemma
64
.

Proof of Lemma
64
.

Let us construct the sequence of functions
\langle f_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa^{+}\rangle
. Fix a
\diamondsuit_{\kappa^{+}}(\cof(\kappa))
-sequence
\langle d_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\kappa^{+}\cap\cof(\kappa)\rangle
. Let
f_{0}:\kappa\to\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\kappa^{+})
be such that for all
i<\kappa
,
f_{0}(i)\leq x_{i}
. Given
f_{\alpha}
, let
f_{\alpha+1}=f_{\alpha}
.
Suppose we have
\langle f_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\rangle
, where
\lambda
is a limit. If
\cf(\lambda)=\delta<\kappa
, let
\langle\alpha_{\beta}:\beta<\delta\rangle
be an increasing cofinal sequence in
\lambda
. Let
i_{0}<\kappa
be such that
f_{\alpha_{\gamma}}(i)\leq f_{\alpha_{\beta}}(i)
whenever
i\geq i_{0}
and
\beta<\gamma<\delta
. Then let
f_{\lambda}(i)=\inf_{\beta<\delta}f_{\alpha_{\beta}}(i)
for
i\geq i_{0}
and
f_{\lambda}(i)=f_{0}(i)
for
i<i_{0}
.

Suppose we have
\langle f_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\rangle
, and
\cf(\lambda)=\kappa
. First, construct a “diagonal lower bound” to the sequence as follows. Let
\langle\alpha_{\beta}:\beta<\kappa\rangle
be a cofinal increasing sequence in
\lambda
. Let
\langle i_{\beta}:\beta<\kappa\rangle\subseteq\kappa
be an increasing continuous sequence such that for
\beta<\gamma<\kappa
and
i\geq i_{\gamma+1}
,
f_{\alpha_{\gamma}}(i)\leq f_{\alpha_{\beta}}(i)
. Then define
f^{\prime}_{\lambda}
by
f^{\prime}_{\lambda}(i)=\inf_{\beta<\gamma}f_{\alpha_{\beta}}(i)
for all
i\in[i_{\gamma},i_{\gamma+1})
and
\gamma<\kappa
. Then we have that for all
\alpha<\lambda
,
f^{\prime}_{\lambda}\leq f_{\alpha}
on a co-bounded set.

Next, examine the set
d_{\lambda}
given by the diamond sequence. Ask whether
d_{\lambda}
codes, via Gödel pairing, a tuple
\langle M,\mathcal{A},\vec{g}\rangle
such that:

1.

M
is a
{<}\kappa
closed transitive model of the theory of
H_{\kappa^{++}}
, with
\lambda=(\kappa^{+})^{M}
.

2.

M\models\mathcal{A}
is a maximal antichain contained in
\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\lambda)
.

3.

\vec{g}\in M
and
\vec{g}=\langle f_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\rangle
.

If this fails, let
f_{\lambda}=f^{\prime}_{\lambda}
. Otherwise, note that by the
{<}\kappa
-closure of
M
,
f^{\prime}_{\lambda}(i)\in M
for all
i<\kappa
. For each
i
, let
f_{\lambda}(i)\in\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\lambda)
be below both
f^{\prime}_{\lambda}(i)
and some
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

To see that we obtain condition (
4
), let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\kappa^{+})
be a maximal antichain. Let
N\prec H_{\kappa^{++}}
be a
\kappa
-closed elementary submodel of size
\kappa^{+}
with
\kappa^{+}\cup\{\langle f_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa^{+}\rangle,\mathcal{A}\}\subseteq N
. Let
D\subseteq\kappa^{+}
code
\langle N,\mathcal{A},\langle f_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa^{+}\rangle\rangle
via Gödel pairing. Let
\langle N_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa^{+}\rangle
be a continuous
\subseteq
-increasing sequence of elemenatary submodels of
N
such that
N=\bigcup_{\alpha}N_{\alpha}
, each
N_{\alpha+1}
is
{<}\kappa
-closed, and
\langle f_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa^{+}\rangle,\mathcal{A}\in N_{0}
.

Since
\langle d_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\kappa^{+}\cap\cof(\kappa)\rangle
is a
\diamondsuit_{\kappa^{+}}(\cof(\kappa))
-sequence, there is some
\lambda\in\kappa^{+}\cap\cof(\kappa)
such that
D\cap\lambda=d_{\lambda}
and
D\cap\lambda
codes the transitive collapse
\langle M,\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\vec{g}\rangle
of
\langle N_{\lambda},\mathcal{A},\langle f_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa^{+}\rangle\rangle
. Note that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
,
\vec{g}=\langle f_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda\rangle
, and
M
is
{<}\kappa
closed since
\cf(\lambda)=\kappa
. Thus by construction, the function
f_{\lambda}
has the property that for all
i<\kappa
,
f_{\lambda}(i)\leq a
for some
a\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
.
∎

This concludes the proof of Theorem
63
.
∎

Corollary 65
.

If
\kappa
is regular,
\diamondsuit_{\kappa^{+}}(\cof(\kappa))
holds, and there is a normal ideal
J
on
\kappa^{+}
such that
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/J\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\kappa,\kappa^{+}))
, then for all regular
\delta\leq\kappa
and all
\delta
-complete uniform ideals
I
on
\kappa
, there is a
\delta
-complete uniform ideal
I^{\prime}
on
\kappa^{+}
such that
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I\cong\mathcal{P}(\kappa^{+})/I^{\prime}
.

Furthermore, there is a function
\pi:\kappa^{+}\to\kappa
such that:

1.

I=\{A\subseteq\kappa:\pi^{-1}[A]\in I^{\prime}\}
.

2.

[A]_{I}\mapsto[\pi^{-1}[A]]_{I^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism from
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I
to
\mathcal{P}(\kappa^{+})/I^{\prime}
.

3.

For
\mu\leq\kappa
,
[f]_{I}\mapsto[f\circ\pi]_{I^{\prime}}
is a bijection from
\mu^{\kappa}/I
to
\mu^{\kappa^{+}}/I^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
63
, there is a function
\pi:\kappa^{+}\to\kappa
and a uniform
\kappa
-complete ideal
K\supseteq J
on
\kappa^{+}
satisfying the conclusions, in particular such that
[A]_{\mathrm{bd}}\mapsto[\pi^{-1}[A]]_{K}
gives an isomorphism
\Phi:\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/\mathrm{bd}\cong\mathcal{P}(\kappa^{+})/K
.
A
\delta
-complete uniform ideal
I
on
\kappa
corresponds to a
\delta
-complete ideal
I_{0}
on the Boolean algebra
\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/\mathrm{bd}
. If
I_{1}=\Phi[I_{0}]
, then
I_{1}
is a
\delta
-complete ideal on
\mathcal{P}(\kappa^{+})/K
, and we can set
I^{\prime}=\{A:[A]_{K}\in I_{1}\}
. We have

\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I\cong(\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/\mathrm{bd})/I_{0}\cong(\mathcal{P}(\kappa^{+})/K)/I_{1}\cong\mathcal{P}(\kappa^{+})/I^{\prime}.

Since both
I_{1}
and
K
are
\delta
-complete, so is
I^{\prime}
.
The “furthermore” part follows from the corresponding part of Theorem
63
and the definition of
I^{\prime}
.
∎

By applying Corollary
65
repeatedly, we obtain the following.

Corollary 66
.

Suppose that for every
n
there is an
\omega_{n+1}
-complete ideal
I_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\omega_{n+1})
such that
\mathcal{P}(\omega_{n+1})/I_{n}\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\omega_{n},\omega_{n+1}))
. Then for every
n\leq m
, there is a uniform,
\omega_{n}
-complete,
\omega_{n}
-dense ideal on
\omega_{m}
.

In particular, the existence of a uniform,
\sigma
-complete,
\omega_{1}
-dense ideal on
\omega_{n+1}
for all
n<\omega
is consistent relative to a huge cardinal.

7.3
Irregular ultrafilters

For any regular cardinal
\kappa
, there exists a sequence
\langle f_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa^{+}\rangle
of functions from
\kappa
to
\kappa
that is increasing modulo bounded sets. Thus for any uniform ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
\kappa
,
|\kappa^{\kappa}/\mathcal{U}|\geq\kappa^{+}
. The same argument shows that for every
\kappa
-decomposible ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
over any set
X
,
17
17

17

An ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
is
\kappa
-decomposible if there are sets
\langle X_{i}:i<\kappa\rangle
such that
\bigcup_{i<\kappa}X_{i}=X
, and for every
\alpha<\kappa
,
\bigcup_{i<\alpha}X_{i}\notin\mathcal{U}
.

|\kappa^{X}/\mathcal{U}|\geq\kappa^{+}
. It is also easy to see that for an ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
X
and a successor cardinal
\kappa=\mu^{+}
,
|\kappa^{X}/\mathcal{U}|\leq|\mu^{X}/\mathcal{U}|^{+}
. Thus for
m\leq n<\omega
, it follows that for all uniform ultrafilters over
\omega_{n}
,
\omega_{m+1}\leq|\omega_{m}^{\omega_{n}}/\mathcal{U}|\leq 2^{\omega_{n}}
.

More generally, Keisler
[
20
]
gave a criterion for when ultrapowers have the maximal possible cardinality.
An ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
is called
(\mu,\kappa)
-regular
when there is a sequence
\langle X_{\alpha}:\alpha<\kappa\rangle\subseteq\mathcal{U}
such that for all
Y\subseteq\kappa
with
\ot(Y)=\mu
,
\bigcap_{\alpha\in Y}X_{\alpha}=\emptyset
. Every countably incomplete ultrafilter is
(\omega,\omega)
-regular, and every uniform ultrafilter on a cardinal
\kappa
is
(\kappa,\kappa)
-regular. For any infinite cardinal
\kappa
, any ultrafilter extending the minimal fine filter on
[\kappa]^{<\omega}
is
(\omega,\kappa)
-regular, so there exists an
(\omega,\kappa)
-regular uniform ultrafilter on
\kappa
.

Theorem 67

(Keisler
[
20
]
)
.

If
\mathcal{U}
is a
(\mu,\kappa)
-regular ultrafilter on
X
, then

|(2^{<\mu})^{X}/\mathcal{U}|\geq 2^{\kappa}.

The existence of irregular ultrafilters — namely uniform ultrafilters on a cardinal
\kappa
which are not
(\omega,\kappa)
-regular — was one of the driving forces behind results regarding strong properties of ideals in the early years of the field. For example, in
[
23
]
, Magidor showed the consistency of the existence of an irregular ultrafilter on
\omega_{2}
from a huge cardinal. This result was improved by Foreman, in
[
12
]
, who showed that from an
\aleph_{1}
-dense ideal on
\omega_{2}
, one can obtain an ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
such that
|\omega^{\omega_{2}}/\mathcal{U}|=\aleph_{1}
, using the transfer principle.

In our model, all possibilities for sizes of ultrapowers on the
\omega_{n}
’s can be realized, subject to the
a priori
constraint given by
\mathrm{GCH}
.

Theorem 68
.

Suppose for all
n<\omega
, there is a normal ideal
I
on
\omega_{n+1}
such that
\mathcal{P}(\omega_{n+1})/I\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\omega_{n},\omega_{n+1}))
. Then for all
a,b,c\in\omega
such that
a<b\leq c+1
, there is a uniform ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
\omega_{c}
such that
|\omega_{a}^{\omega_{c}}/\mathcal{U}|=\omega_{b}
.

Proof.

Note that the hypothesis implies
\mathrm{GCH}
below
\aleph_{\omega}
by Proposition
57
.

We argue by induction on
c
. If
c=0
, then
a=0
and
b=1
. Any non-principal ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
\omega
has the property that

|\omega^{\omega}/\mathcal{U}|=2^{\omega}=\omega_{1}
.

Suppose the conclusion holds when
c<n
. To realize
|\omega_{a}^{\omega_{n}}/\mathcal{U}|=\omega_{n+1}
for
a\leq n
, choose any
(\omega,\omega_{n})
-regular uniform ultrafilter on
\omega_{n}
. To obtain
|\omega_{a}^{\omega_{n}}/\mathcal{U}|=\omega_{b}
for
a<b\leq n
, we have by induction that there is a uniform ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}_{0}
on
\omega_{n-1}
such that
|\omega_{a}^{\omega_{n-1}}/\mathcal{U}_{0}|=\omega_{b}
. By Corollary
65
, there is a uniform ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}_{1}
on
\omega_{n}
such that

|\omega_{a}^{\omega_{n}}/\mathcal{U}_{1}|=|\omega_{a}^{\omega_{n-1}}/\mathcal{U}_{0}|
.
∎

7.4
The Foreman-Kunen spectrum

In
[
13
]
, Foreman generalized an unpublished result of Kunen that there is no uniform, countably complete,
\omega_{2}
-saturated ideal on any cardinal in the interval
[\aleph_{\omega},\aleph_{\omega_{1}})
. For a regular cardinal
\kappa
, Foreman defined the collection
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
as the smallest class such that:

1.

\kappa\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
.

2.

If
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
and
\beta^{+\alpha}\geq\kappa^{+\omega}
, then
\beta^{+\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
.

3.

If
\beta\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
and
\beta\geq\kappa
, then every cardinal in the interval
[\beta,\beta^{+\kappa})
is in
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
.

(Note that (3) actually follows from (1) and (2) since we can show by induction that
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}\cap[\kappa,\kappa^{+\omega})=\emptyset
and apply (2) for each
\alpha<\kappa
.)
He showed that if
\kappa
is a successor cardinal, then there is no uniform,
\kappa
-complete,
\kappa^{+}
-saturated ideal on any
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
. He asked whether
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
fully describes the extent of the limitation. Specifically, is it consistent that for all regular
\lambda\notin\mathcal{C}_{\omega_{1}}
, there is a uniform, countably complete,
\omega_{2}
-saturated ideal on
\lambda
?

In
[
14
]
, Foreman emphasized the question of whether the lower end of this restriction is sharp. The present work shows that it is. It follows by Corollary
65
that in our model, for every pair of positive natural numbers
n\leq m
, there is a uniform,
\omega_{n}
-complete,
\omega_{n}
-dense ideal on
\omega_{m}
.

We would also like to answer Foreman’s question about
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
by showing that it is actually not restrictive enough. There are further cardinals on which there is provably no uniform, countably complete,
\omega_{2}
-saturated ideal.

Definition 69
.

Let
\Gamma
be a class of forcings.

The
Foreman-Kunen spectrum

\mathsf{FK}(\kappa,\Gamma)
is the class of regular cardinals
\lambda
such that there exists a uniform,
\kappa
-complete,
\kappa^{+}
-incomplete, precipitous ideal
I
on
\lambda
with the property that
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)/I\in\Gamma
.

We define
\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
as the collection of ordinals
\lambda
such that for every
\mathbb{P}\in\Gamma
, if
\mathbb{P}
forces an elementary embedding
j:V\to M\subseteq V[G]
, where
M
is transitive and
\crit(j)=\kappa
, then
j(\lambda)=\lambda
.
18
18

18

To render this collection first-order definable, we should restrict to embeddings with domain some
V_{\theta}
,
\theta>\kappa+\lambda
, and codomain some transitive set
M\in V[G]
.

Foreman’s argument shows that if
\kappa
is a successor cardinal and
\Gamma
is the class of
\kappa^{+}
-c.c. forcings, then
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}\subseteq\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
.

Proposition 70
.

\mathsf{FK}(\kappa,\Gamma)\cap\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

For any regular cardinal
\lambda
, there is a sequence of functions
\langle f_{\alpha}:\alpha<\lambda^{+}\rangle
from
\lambda
to
\lambda
that is increasing modulo bounded sets. If
\lambda\in\mathsf{FK}(\kappa,\Gamma)
, then there is a uniform
\kappa
-complete precipitous ideal
I
on
\lambda

such that
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)/I\in\Gamma
.
If
G\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\lambda)/I
is generic, then let
j:V\to M\subseteq V[G]
be the ultrapower embedding. Then
[f_{\alpha}]_{G}<[f_{\beta}]_{G}
for all
\alpha<\beta<\lambda^{+}
. Thus
j(\lambda)\geq(\lambda^{+})^{V}
.
∎

For a class of forcings
\Gamma
, let us say that a property

\varphi(x)
is
\Gamma
-robust above
\delta
if every forcing
\mathbb{P}\in\Gamma
preserves
\varphi(\alpha)
for every ordinal
\alpha\geq\delta
.
If
\Gamma
is a class of forcings of size less than
\delta
, then all standard large cardinal notions are
\Gamma
-robust above
\delta
.

Proposition 71
.

Suppose
\kappa
is a successor cardinal,
\Gamma
is a class of
\kappa^{+\omega}
-c.c. forcings, and
\varphi(x)
is a
\Pi_{1}
formula that is
\Gamma
-robust above
\kappa^{+\omega}
.

1.

\kappa\cup\{\kappa^{+\omega}\}\subseteq\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
.

2.

If
\alpha,\beta\in\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
,
\beta\geq\kappa^{+\omega}
, and
\delta
is the
\alpha^{th}
ordinal above
\beta
at which
\varphi(x)
holds, then
\delta\in\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
.

3.

If
\alpha
is a limit point of
\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
such that
\cf(\alpha)\in\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
, then
\alpha\in\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
.

Before giving the proof, let us compare this claim with Foreman’s result about
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
. Since being a cardinal is a
\Pi_{1}
property that is
\Gamma
-robust above
\kappa^{+\omega}
for any
\Gamma
contained in the class of
\kappa^{+\omega}
-c.c. forcings, the above proposition implies that
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}\subseteq\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
for successor
\kappa
. The class
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
can be formed as an increasing
\omega
-sequence in the following way. Let
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}^{0}=\kappa
, and given
\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\kappa}
, let

\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}^{n+1}=\{\beta^{+\alpha}:\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}^{n}\wedge\beta^{+\alpha}\notin[\kappa,\kappa^{+\omega})\}.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}=\bigcup_{n}\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}^{n}
. If
\kappa
is a successor cardinal, then
\sup(\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}^{1})=\kappa^{+\kappa}=\aleph_{\kappa}>\kappa
.
Assume
n\geq 2
and for
1\leq i<n
,
\delta_{i}=\sup(\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}^{i})
is a limit cardinal, and
\delta_{i}=\aleph_{\delta_{i-1}}>\delta_{i-1}
.
Then

\delta_{n}:=\sup\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}^{n}=\sup\{\beta^{+\alpha}:\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}^{n-1}\}=\aleph_{\delta_{n-1}}>\aleph_{\delta_{n-2}}=\delta_{n-1}

Thus if
\delta=\sup_{n}\delta_{n}
, then
\sup\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}=\delta=\aleph_{\delta}
, and this is the first fixed point of the
\aleph
-function above
\kappa
. However, note that being a fixed point of the
\aleph
-function is a
\Pi_{1}
property that is
\Gamma
-robust above
\kappa^{+\omega}
when
\Gamma
is a class of
\kappa^{+\omega}
-c.c. forcings. Also,
\delta
is a limit of
\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
of countable cofinality. Thus
\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
is strictly larger than
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
for such
\Gamma
. Other examples of
\Pi_{1}
properties that are
\Gamma
-robust for suitable
\Gamma
above sufficiently large ordinals include being regular, being a limit cardinal, and some degrees of (weak) Mahloness.

Proof of Proposition
71
.

First note that
\kappa\subseteq\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
by definition.
Next note that if
\kappa
is a successor cardinal and
j:V\to M\subseteq V[G]
is a generic embedding with critical point
\kappa
, where
V[G]
is
a
\kappa^{+\omega}
-c.c. forcing extension and
M
is transitive, then
\kappa^{+\omega}
is closed under
j
. This holds because otherwise, if we take the least
n
such that
j(\kappa^{+n})>\kappa^{+\omega}
, then we would have a tail of cardinals below
\kappa^{+\omega}
that are forced to have the same cardinality as
j(\mu)
, where
\kappa^{+n}=\mu^{+}
. It follows that
\kappa^{+\omega}\in\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
.

Suppose
\alpha,\beta\in\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
,
\beta\geq\kappa^{+\omega}
, and
\delta
is the
\alpha^{th}
ordinal above
\beta
at which
\varphi(x)
holds. Suppose
\mathbb{P}\in\Gamma
,
G\subseteq\mathbb{P}
is generic over
V
, and
j:V\to M\subseteq V[G]
is an elementary embedding with
M
transitive and
\crit(j)=\kappa
.
Then
M\models
“
j(\delta)
is the
j(\alpha)^{th}
ordinal
\gamma\geq j(\beta)\geq j(\kappa^{+\omega})
such that
\varphi(\gamma)
,” and note that
j
is the identity on
\alpha,\beta,\kappa^{+\omega}
. Since
\varphi(x)
is
\Gamma
-robust above
\kappa^{+\omega}
and a
\Pi_{1}
property,

\{\gamma\geq\kappa^{+\omega}:V\models\varphi(\gamma)\}=\{\gamma\geq\kappa^{+\omega}:V[G]\models\varphi(\gamma)\}.

Again because
\varphi(x)
is
\Pi_{1}
,

\{\gamma\geq\kappa^{+\omega}:M\models\varphi(\gamma)\}\supseteq\{\gamma\geq\kappa^{+\omega}:V[G]\models\varphi(\gamma)\}.

Thus the
\alpha^{th}
ordinal
\gamma\geq\beta
such that
M\models\varphi(\gamma)
, is less than or equal to the
\alpha^{th}
ordinal
\gamma\geq\beta
such that
V[G]\models\varphi(\gamma)
. Thus
j(\delta)\leq\delta
, so
j(\delta)=\delta
.

For the final claim, suppose
\alpha
is a limit point of
\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
and
\cf(\alpha)\in\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
. Let
\langle\alpha_{i}:i<\cf(\alpha)\rangle
be an increasing sequence contained in
\mathrm{fix}(\kappa,\Gamma)
and cofinal in
\alpha
.
Suppose
\mathbb{P}\in\Gamma
,
G\subseteq\mathbb{P}
is generic over
V
, and
j:V\to M\subseteq V[G]
is an elementary embedding with
M
transitive and
\crit(j)=\kappa
.
Then

j(\langle\alpha_{i}:i<\cf(\alpha)\rangle)=\langle\beta_{i}:i<j(\cf(\alpha))\rangle=\langle\beta_{i}:i<\cf(\alpha)\rangle

is cofinal in
j(\alpha)
, and

\langle j(\alpha_{i}):i<\cf(\alpha)\rangle=\langle\alpha_{i}:i<\cf(\alpha)\rangle

is cofinal in
\langle\beta_{i}:i<\cf(\alpha)\rangle
. Thus
j(\alpha)=\sup_{i}\beta_{i}=\sup_{i}\alpha_{i}=\alpha
.
∎

The restriction to
\Pi_{1}
properties is important, as can be seen by the
\Sigma_{2}
property of being a measurable cardinal. If
\kappa
is a successor cardinal, there is a
\kappa^{+}
-saturated normal ideal on
\kappa
, and
\lambda>\kappa
is measurable, then by Proposition
62
, there is a
\kappa
-complete uniform ideal
J
on
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)/J\cong\mathcal{P}(\kappa)/I
. Thus
\lambda\in\mathsf{FK}(\kappa,\kappa^{+}\text{-c.c.})
. By the same token, if
\lambda>\kappa
is strongly compact, then every regular
\delta\geq\lambda
is in
\mathsf{FK}(\kappa,\kappa^{+}\text{-c.c.})
.

It is natural to ask exactly how large
\mathsf{FK}(\kappa,\Gamma)
can be.
Foreman
[
13
]
showed that consistently,
\mathsf{FK}(\kappa,\kappa\text{-centered})
contains
\{\lambda^{+}:\lambda=|\lambda|>\cf(\lambda)=\kappa\}
simultaneously for all successor cardinals
\kappa
.

7.5
Graph colorings

A
graph
is a pair
G=\langle V_{G},E_{G}\rangle
, where
E_{G}\subseteq[V_{G}]^{2}
.
V_{G}
is the set of
vertices
, and
E_{G}
is the set of
edges
. For graphs
G,H
, we say that
H
is a
subgraph
of
G
when
V_{H}\subseteq V_{G}
and
E_{H}=E_{G}\cap[V_{H}]^{2}
.

A
vertex coloring
of a graph
G
is a function
c:V_{G}\to\kappa
for some cardinal
\kappa
such that
c(x)\not=c(y)
whenever
\{x,y\}\in E_{G}
. The
chromatic number
of a graph
G
, or
\chi(G)
, is the least cardinal
\kappa
such that there exists a vertex coloring of
G
with range
\kappa
.

Theorem 72

(Erdős-Hajnal
[
6
]
)
.

For every infinite cardinal
\kappa
and every
n<\omega
, there exists a graph
G
with
\beth_{n}(\kappa)^{+}
-many vertices such that
\chi(G)>\kappa
, but
\chi(H)\leq\kappa
for every subgraph
H
of
G
such that
|V_{H}|\leq\beth_{n}(\kappa)
.

Here, we use the standard notation that
\beth_{0}(\kappa)=\kappa
, and
\beth_{n+1}(\kappa)=2^{\beth_{n}(\kappa)}
.

In
[
6
]
, Erdős and Hajnal also introduced their universal graph
\mathsf{EH}(\kappa,\lambda)
, defined by taking the vertex set as the set of all functions
f:\kappa\to\lambda
, and connecting
f,g
with an edge when
|\{\alpha:f(\alpha)=g(\alpha)\}|<\kappa
. (So two functions are connected when they
dis
agree almost everywhere.) They showed that for every graph
G
with vertex set of size
\kappa
, if every subgraph
H
with
|V_{H}|<|V_{G}|
has
\chi(H)\leq\lambda
, then there is an edge-preserving homomorphism
f:G\to\mathsf{EH}(\kappa,\lambda)
. This implies that
\chi(G)\leq\chi(\mathsf{EH}(\kappa,\lambda))
.
By Theorem
72
, we have that for every infinite
\kappa
and
n<\omega
,

\chi(\mathsf{EH}(\beth_{n}(\kappa)^{+},\kappa))>\kappa
.

Because of their usefulness in computing the chromatic numbers of infinite graphs in general, several authors, such as Todorčević
[
30
]
, have urged the computation of the chromatic numbers of these graphs. We note that
[
21
]
shows that the value of
\chi(\mathsf{EH}(\omega_{2},\omega))
is independent of
\mathrm{ZFC}+\mathrm{GCH}
.

In our model, the value of
\chi(\mathsf{EH}(\omega_{m},\omega_{n}))
equals the lower bound given by Erdős and Hajnal for all
n\leq m<\omega
.

Theorem 73
.

Suppose for all
n<\omega
, there is a normal ideal
I
on
\omega_{n+1}
such that
\mathcal{P}(\omega_{n+1})/I\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\omega_{n},\omega_{n+1}))
.
Then for all
n\leq m<\omega
,
\chi(\mathsf{EH}(\omega_{m},\omega_{n}))=\omega_{n+1}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
68
, there exists a uniform ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
\omega_{m}
such that
|\omega_{n}^{\omega_{m}}/\mathcal{U}|=\omega_{n+1}
. We can color the vertices of
\mathsf{EH}(\omega_{m},\omega_{n})
by their equivalence classes mod
\mathcal{U}
. If
f
and
g
are connected with an edge, then they disagree on a cobounded set, so
[f]_{\mathcal{U}}\not=[g]_{\mathcal{U}}
. Since the hypothesis implies
\mathrm{GCH}
below
\aleph_{\omega}
,
\chi(\mathsf{EH}(\omega_{m},\omega_{n}))\geq\omega_{n+1}
, so this is an optimal vertex coloring.
∎

In our model, we obtain many instances of the Foreman-Laver graph reflection property of
[
11
,
15
]
. Recall that
[\kappa,\lambda]\twoheadrightarrow[\kappa^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}]
means that every graph with
\kappa
vertices and chromatic number
\lambda
has a subgraph with
\kappa^{\prime}
vertices and chromatic number
\lambda^{\prime}
. We write
[\kappa,{\geq}\lambda]\twoheadrightarrow[\kappa^{\prime},{\geq}\lambda^{\prime}]
to mean that the chromatic numbers are not assumed to be exactly
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
respectively, but bounded below by these cardinals.

Corollary 74
.

Suppose for all
n<\omega
, there is a normal ideal
I
on
\omega_{n+1}
such that
\mathcal{P}(\omega_{n+1})/I\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\omega_{n},\omega_{n+1}))
.
Then for all natural numbers
k,m,n
with
n\leq m
,

[\omega_{m+k},\omega_{m}]\twoheadrightarrow[\omega_{n+k},\omega_{n}].

Proof.

We first establish
[\omega_{m+k},\geq\!\omega_{m}]\twoheadrightarrow[\omega_{n+k},\geq\!\omega_{n}]
for all
k,m,n
with
n\leq m
. It suffices to assume that
m=n+1
, since then the other instances follow from the transitivity of the transfer property.

First, let us also assume that
n\geq 1
. Suppose
G
is a graph such that
|V_{G}|=\omega_{m+k}
and
\chi(G)\geq\omega_{m}
. If the transfer property fails, then for every subgraph
H
such that
|V_{H}|\leq\omega_{n+k}=\omega_{m+k-1}
,
\chi(H)\leq\omega_{n-1}
. But then there is an edge-preserving homomorphism
f:G\to\mathsf{EH}(\omega_{m+k},\omega_{n-1})
. Since
\chi(\mathsf{EH}(\omega_{m+k},\omega_{n-1}))=\omega_{n}=\omega_{m-1}
,
\chi(G)<\omega_{m}
, a contradiction.

If
n=0
, then let
G
be a graph with
V_{G}=\omega_{m+k}
and
\chi(G)\geq\omega_{m}
, and let
M
be an elementary substructure of
\langle H_{\omega_{m+k+1}},\in,G\rangle
of size
\omega_{k}
. Let
H
be the induced subgraph with
V_{H}=V_{G}\cap M
. By the De Bruijn–Erdős theorem, having infinite chromatic number is a first-order property, so indeed
\chi(H)\geq\omega
.

For the stronger property
[\omega_{m+k},\omega_{m}]\twoheadrightarrow[\omega_{n+k},\omega_{n}]
, we argue by induction on
k
. First note that the stronger relation is same as the weaker one when
k=0
.
Suppose that
k>0
and
[\omega_{m+k^{\prime}},\omega_{m}]\twoheadrightarrow[\omega_{n+k^{\prime}},\omega_{n}]
holds for all
n\leq m<\omega
and
k^{\prime}<k
. Let
G
be any graph on
\omega_{m+k}
with
\chi(G)=\omega_{m}
. Then there is an independent set of vertices,
X
, of size
\omega_{m+k}
. Let
H_{0}
be a subgraph witnessing
[\omega_{m+k},\geq\!\omega_{m}]\twoheadrightarrow[\omega_{n+k},\geq\!\omega_{n}]
.
If
\chi(H_{0})=\omega_{n}
, we are done. If
\chi(H_{0})=\omega_{n+r}
where
0<r\leq k
, then by induction we have
[\omega_{n+k},\omega_{n+r}]\twoheadrightarrow[\omega_{n+k-r},\omega_{n}]
.
Let
H_{1}
be a subgraph of
H_{0}
witnessing this relation.
Then let
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
have size
\omega_{n+k}
, and let
H
be the subgraph of
G
with
V_{H}=V_{H_{1}}\cup X^{\prime}
. Since
X^{\prime}
is independent, any vertex coloring of
H_{1}
can be extended to one of
H
by using only one more color for the members of
X^{\prime}\setminus H_{1}
. Thus
|V_{H}|=\omega_{n+k}
, and
\chi(H)=\chi(H_{1})=\omega_{n}
.
∎

We note that the conclusion above is optimal in a sense. One the one hand, if
k>0
and
[\omega_{m+k},\omega_{m}]\twoheadrightarrow[\omega_{n+k},\omega_{n}]
holds, then also
[\omega_{m+k},\omega_{m}]\twoheadrightarrow[\omega_{r+k},\omega_{n}]
holds for
n\leq r\leq m
, which can be seen adjoining a large enough set of independent vertices to a witness of the first relation. In other words, a nonzero gap between cardinality and chromatic number can be increased in a subgraph. However, it cannot generally be decreased.
19
19

19

This contrasts to the cosmetically similar Chang’s Conjecture
(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{0})\twoheadrightarrow(\mu_{1},\mu_{0})
about elementary submodels, wherein the gap can be readily decreased but not increased.
This is shown by the construction of Erdős and Hajnal
[
6
]
. For a set of ordinals
A
and
n\geq 2
, they define a graph
G_{n}(A)
on
[A]^{n}
by connecting two nodes
\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\},\{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n}\}
(written in increasing order) when
\beta_{i}=\alpha_{i+1}
for
0<i<n
. They show that, assuming
\mathrm{GCH}
holds below
\aleph_{\omega}
, for all
n,k<\omega
such that
k\geq 1
, if
|A|=\omega_{n+k}
, then
\chi(G_{k+1}(A))=\omega_{n}
.
Therefore, under
\mathrm{GCH}
, if
n\leq m
,
k\geq 1
, and
H
is a subgraph of
G_{k+1}(\omega_{m+k})
of chromatic number
\omega_{n}
, then
|V_{H}|\geq\omega_{n+k}
. Thus for
n\leq m
and
k_{0}<k_{1}
,
[\omega_{m+k_{1}},\omega_{m}]\twoheadrightarrow[\omega_{n+k_{0}},\omega_{n}]
is inconsistent with
\mathrm{GCH}
.

In contrast to the results above, Shelah
[
25
]
showed that in
L
, for every successor cardinal
\kappa
, there is a graph
G
with
|V_{G}|=\kappa
and
\chi(G)=\kappa
such that for every subgraph
H
with
|V_{H}|<\kappa
,
\chi(H)\leq\omega
. A similar result was obtained by Shelah (using a non-reflecting stationary set and instances of
\mathrm{GCH}
, with larger graphs) in
[
26
]
and by Lambie-Hanson and Rinot
[
22
]
, using
\square(\kappa)
and
\mathrm{GCH}
, obtaining various gaps.

7.6
Partition hypotheses

In recent work
[
2
]
, Bannister, Bergfalk, Moore, and Todorčević isolated a combinatorial principle
\mathrm{PH}_{n}(\kappa)
.
To state this principle, let us first define a few notions. For finite sequences
s,t
, we put
s\lhd t
when
s
is a strict subsequence of
t
.
For
1\leq n<\omega
, a function
F:\kappa^{\leq n}\to\kappa
is called
n
-cofinal
when

•

for all
\alpha<\kappa
,
F(\langle\alpha\rangle)\geq\alpha
, and

•

whenever
s,t\in\kappa^{\leq n}
and
s\lhd t
,
F(s)\leq F(t)
.

\mathrm{PH}_{n}(\kappa)
states that for all colorings
c:\kappa^{n+1}\to\omega
, there is
m<\omega
and an
(n+1)
-cofinal
F:\kappa^{\leq n+1}\to\kappa
such that for all sequences
s_{1}\lhd s_{2}\lhd\dots\lhd s_{n+1}
such that
s_{i}\in\kappa^{i}
,

c\left(F(s_{1}),F(s_{2}),\dots,F(s_{n+1})\right)=m.

The combinatorial power of
\mathrm{PH}_{n}(\kappa)
stems in part from the fact that for any injective sequence
\langle\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{n+1}\rangle\subseteq\kappa
, there are
(n+1)!
ways to arrange subsequences
s_{1}\lhd s_{2}\lhd\dots\lhd s_{n+1}=\langle\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{n+1}\rangle
with
\len(s_{1})=1
, and
c\left(F(s_{1}),F(s_{2}),\dots,F(s_{n+1})\right)
will be the same for any such arrangement.

They showed the following results:

1.

For all
n
,
\mathrm{PH}_{n}(\omega_{n})
is false.

2.

If
\kappa
is regular and
n\geq 1
, then
\mathrm{PH}_{n}(\kappa)
implies
\neg\square(\kappa)
.

3.

If
\kappa
is weakly compact, then
\mathrm{PH}_{n}(\kappa)
holds for all
n
.

4.

If
1\leq n<m
and for
1\leq k\leq n
, there is a uniform,
\omega_{k}
-complete,
\omega_{k}
-dense ideal on
\omega_{m}
, then
\mathrm{PH}_{n}(\omega_{m})
holds.

Our model thus answers their question of whether
\mathrm{PH}_{n}(\omega_{m})
is consistent for
2\leq n<m
.

In more recent work in preparation
[
3
]
, Berkfalk, Lambie-Hanson, and Zhang introduce a similar principle,
\mathrm{PH}^{\mathrm{bd}}_{n}(\kappa,\mathrm{reg})
. This states that for all
c:\kappa^{n+1}\to\kappa
such that
c(s)<\min(s)
for all
s\in\kappa^{n+1}
, there is an
(n+1)
-cofinal
F:\kappa^{\leq n+1}\to\kappa
and an ordinal
\xi<\kappa
such that for all sequences
s_{1}\lhd s_{2}\lhd\dots\lhd s_{n+1}
such that
s_{i}\in\kappa^{i}
,

c\left(F(s_{1}),F(s_{2}),\dots,F(s_{n+1})\right)<\xi.

They apply this principle in the study of homological algebra. There are some rather concretely defined groups of families of functions on ordinals into a fixed abelian group such that the question of whether certain quotient groups are trivial, namely the subgroup of “
n
-coherent” families modulo the subgroup of “
n
-trivial” families, is independent of
\mathrm{ZFC}
. For a given ordinal
\delta
and fixed abelian group
\mathcal{A}
, this quotient group is denoted by
\check{\mathrm{H}}^{n}(\delta,\mathcal{A})
. They show that for
m<n
,
\check{\mathrm{H}}^{n}(\omega_{m},\mathcal{A})
is always trivial,
\check{\mathrm{H}}^{n}(\omega_{n},\mathcal{A})
is nontrivial for some choice of
\mathcal{A}
, and the status of
\check{\mathrm{H}}^{n}(\omega_{m},\mathcal{A})
for
n<m
is independent. They show that the axiom
V=L
implies that
\check{\mathrm{H}}^{n}(\omega_{m},\mathcal{A})\neq 0
whenever
n<m
, that
\mathrm{PFA}
implies that
\check{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(\omega_{m},\mathcal{A})=0
when
m\geq 2
, and that the existence of the kind of uniform ideals on
\omega_{m}
in our model implies that
\check{\mathrm{H}}^{n}(\omega_{m},\mathcal{A})=0
whenever
n<m
. This argument goes through the principle
\mathrm{PH}^{\mathrm{bd}}_{n}(\kappa,\mathrm{reg})
and is currently the only known way to show the consistency of
\check{\mathrm{H}}^{n}(\omega_{m},\mathcal{A})=0
for
2\leq n<m
. For more details, see
[
3
]
.

In summary, we have:

Theorem 75
.

Suppose
\mathrm{ZFC}
is consistent with a huge cardinal. Then there is a model of
\mathrm{ZFC}
in which:

1.

\mathrm{PH}_{n}(\omega_{m})
holds for all
n<m<\omega
.

2.

For
n,m\in\omega
,
n=m
if and only if there exists an abelian group
\mathcal{A}
such that the cohomology group
\check{\mathrm{H}}^{n}(\omega_{m},\mathcal{A})
is nontrivial.

8
Open Questions

We conclude this paper with a list of open questions.

In
[
14
]
, Foreman asks whether the existence of strongly layered ideal on each of the
\aleph_{n}
implies that
\aleph_{\omega}
is Jónsson. If true, it would imply that
\aleph_{\omega}
is Jónsson in the model obtained in this paper and in particular that many instances of Chang’s Conjecture hold in this model.

Question 1
.

Assume that for all
n<\omega
there is a normal
I
ideal on
\omega_{n+1}
such that
\mathcal{P}(\omega_{n+1})/I\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\omega_{n},\omega_{n+1}))
.

Are there
0<k<n<m
such that
(\omega_{m},\omega_{n})\twoheadrightarrow(\omega_{n},\omega_{k})
?

Question 2
.

Assume that for all
n<\omega
, there is a normal
I
ideal on
\omega_{n+1}
such that
\mathcal{P}(\omega_{n+1})/I\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{Col}(\omega_{n},\omega_{n+1}))
.

1.

Does
\mathrm{SCH}
hold at
\aleph_{\omega}
?

2.

Can there be an
\aleph_{\omega+2}
-saturated ideal on
\aleph_{\omega+1}
?

3.

Can there be an
\omega_{1}
-indecomposible ultrafilter on
\aleph_{\omega+1}
?

The following question uses the notations of Subsection
7.4
.

Question 3
.

Is it consistent that for every regular cardinal
\kappa
below the first
\aleph
-fixed point that is not in
\mathrm{fix}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\text{-c.c.})
, there is a uniform,
\aleph_{1}
-dense, countably complete ideal on
\kappa
?

The forcing
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
and the anonymous collapse
\mathbb{A}(\kappa,\lambda)
share many interesting properties. In particular, by the minimality of the anonymous collapse, there is a projection from
\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda)
to
\mathbb{A}(\kappa,\lambda)
.

Question 4
.

Let
\kappa
be regular uncountable cardinal,
\lambda>\kappa
inaccessible. Is
\mathcal{B}(\text{\char 87\relax}(\kappa,\lambda))\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{A}(\kappa,\lambda))
?
